AML Trial Watch

Daily research watch · acute myeloid leukemia

AML Trial Watch

For a woman of 84 on first-line, non-intensive treatment in the Seattle area. Her findings: FLT3 D835Y (6%) and FLT3-ITD; DDX41 R525H (45%) and M1? (42%, possibly inherited); DNMT3A R882H. Everything here is a question for her care team, not a recommendation.

Newest report: the 2026-11-02, 12:09 UTC runPage refreshed 2026-11-02 18:32 Pacific517 AML studies tracked, 410 relevant
Open questions11 open

  1. How many cycles of venetoclax and azacitidine has she completed, and when did she start? Oncologist

    She is in her first cycle (Oct 31). Several trials allow patients already on these drugs only within a limit, such as one cycle (NCT06696183) or exactly two (NCT04588922), so the count matters month by month. Still open: the start date. Review notes

  2. Is her azacitidine supply or schedule affected by the shortage? Infusion center

    Four injection presentations are listed unavailable and one limited. Shortage listings

  3. Was she tested for NPM1 and KMT2A? Oncologist

    If both are negative, the three menin-inhibitor trials near the top of the list can be set aside.

  4. Should she have germline DDX41 testing and see a genetic counselor? Oncologist

    The M1? variant at 42% may be inherited, which matters for her children. ClinVar (Nov 2) now lists the matching start-codon change as pathogenic or likely pathogenic, several labs, no conflicts. For the family

  5. What is her FLT3-ITD allelic ratio? Her lab report

    The report confirms an ITD but leaves the ratio in a comment that wasn't included. Several FLT3 trials and treatment choices depend on it.

  6. After treatment, how will her residual disease be measured, given her DNMT3A mutation? Oncologist

    NCT07645469 (WT1 T cells with azacitidine, Fred Hutch) does not accept TET2, ASXL1 or DNMT3A mutations as the only evidence of residual disease, and she has DNMT3A R882H. It does measure FLT3-ITD residual disease, so if her ITD can be tracked, the DNMT3A rule may not shut her out. Ask whether her ITD will be followed after treatment. NCT07645469

  7. Did her mutation panel test IDH1 and IDH2, and were both negative? Her lab report

    Several trials need an IDH1 or IDH2 mutation. The first review assumed her panel tested both and found neither; the Opus second opinion pointed out her profile does not say so. NCT04603001

  8. Is NCT07094750, the Fred Hutch study that randomizes less fit adults between lower- and higher-intensity treatment, open to someone who has already started venetoclax? Oncologist

    Fred Hutch and UW, no upper age limit; patients 75 or older need ECOG 0 to 2. Claude read it on Nov 1 as depends: it is unclear whether starting venetoclax and azacitidine first rules a patient out. NCT07094750

  9. With both FLT3-ITD and D835Y, would a quizartinib-based trial suit her less than a gilteritinib-based one? Oncologist

    CIViC lists laboratory evidence (level D) that D835Y lowers sensitivity to quizartinib, while gilteritinib has level A evidence in relapsed FLT3-mutated AML. Quizartinib trials in the watch include NCT07478991 (VENP-A-QUI). CIViC table

  10. If gilteritinib is added after the first round, which trials would that rule out, and is any of them worth asking about first? Oncologist

    11 tracked trials exclude a prior FLT3 inhibitor, including NCT06696183 (gilteritinib with venetoclax and azacitidine, one prior cycle allowed). Others are written for patients after a FLT3 inhibitor. Trials

  11. Is the Fred Hutch phone-app study for patients starting AML treatment (NCT06472128) open to her, or has her first cycle passed its window? Oncologist

    Enrolls adults 18 to 120 who are starting intensive chemotherapy or a hypomethylating agent, are hospitalized and speak English. Claude read it on Nov 1 as depends on timing. NCT06472128

Review notes

16 items, written 2026-11-02

Nov 2: a quiet day for trials. No relevant changes in either data run, and Claude read 3 newly due trials (about 0.1% of a 5-hour session), none clear. The new observational search near Seattle found its first 3 studies; none fits her (cord blood for transplant, a Down syndrome survivors study, and one already tracked). ClinVar now classifies her DDX41 start-codon change (M1?) as pathogenic or likely pathogenic, with several labs agreeing and no conflicts, which makes the germline-testing question more pressing. New early papers include a STAG2 finding after low-intensity induction and a review of unfit IDH1-mutated AML; neither is about her variants. Health Canada's trial database is now a source too: AML trials authorized in Canada appear under Trials with the tracked trials that use the same drug, from the first data run after the code is pushed.

Nov 2: first observational results, and a firmer DDX41 classification

The observational search added yesterday returned 3 studies near Seattle. NCT01351545 distributes unlicensed cord blood units for transplant (Claude: depends, only if a cord blood transplant were planned). NCT05702645 is for Down syndrome leukemia survivors aged 6 to 39 (blocker). The third, NCT05334069, was already tracked. ClinVar updated the germline classification of DDX41 c.2T>C (p.Met1Thr), a start-codon change of the kind listed as her M1?, from pathogenic to pathogenic or likely pathogenic, now with several submitting labs and no conflicts. At 42% this change may be inherited, which bears on family members and on any related donor. Not yet in PubMed: STAG2 mutation and better survival after transplant following low-intensity induction (Blood Cancer Journal), and a review of the care path for unfit patients with IDH1-mutated AML. Question for her oncologist: given the firmer ClinVar classification, should germline DDX41 testing and genetic counseling happen now?

ClinVar DDX41 p.Met1Thr NCT01351545 STAG2 paper Unfit IDH1m AML review Observational studies

Nov 1: first reading of the new search lists

This morning's data run filled the four searches added on Oct 31: 134 trials filed under MDS whose criteria mention AML, 66 studies on stopping or shortening venetoclax, 5 strategy and care studies near Seattle, and 24 FDA labels for AML. Claude read the 60 enrolling trials it had not seen before (about 1.4% of a 5-hour session): 43 blockers and 17 depends, none clear. Most depends verdicts are transplant studies that need a remission and a donor (for example NCT07025824, NCT06904482, NCT07249346), or trials far from Seattle. Two near Seattle stand out because they enroll at the start of treatment. NCT06472128 at Fred Hutch tests a phone app for patients starting AML treatment, including a hypomethylating agent, ages 18 to 120; it asks for patients who are hospitalized and speak English, and her first cycle may already be past its window. NCT07094750, also at Fred Hutch, randomizes less fit adults between lower- and higher-intensity treatment; patients 75 or older need ECOG 0 to 2, and it is unclear whether having started venetoclax and azacitidine rules her out. Further away: NCT06599762 tests tranexamic acid for bleeding in AML and MDS with very low platelets, and NCT07528417 tests antibiotic prevention for patients starting a hypomethylating agent (not yet recruiting). Question for her oncologist: is either Fred Hutch study open to a patient treated at another Seattle center who has already started venetoclax and azacitidine?

NCT06472128 NCT07094750 NCT06599762 NCT07528417 Review

Update from her oncologist, and what it changes

Relayed by family on Oct 31: she is in her first round of venetoclax and azacitidine (so azacitidine, not decitabine). Gilteritinib, a FLT3 inhibitor, may be added after the first round, depending on her blood counts and how she feels. She was considered for the first-round trial and did not qualify, and her oncologist knows of no other trial for her profile locally in the near future. For the watch this moves the focus to the next step. Once gilteritinib is started, trials that exclude a prior FLT3 inhibitor close to her (11 tracked, for example NCT06696183, which has no upper age limit), while trials written for patients after venetoclax or a FLT3 inhibitor become the likely options (among those with no upper age limit: NCT06191263, RVU120 with venetoclax after venetoclax and an HMA; NCT07813390, after venetoclax failure, by invitation; and the gilteritinib-failure group of NCT06366789, with a site at OHSU in Portland). None of these has a site in Seattle. Each trial now shows these as amber "possible requirement" marks found by text pattern, and Claude's next readings take the new answers into account. Question for her oncologist: before gilteritinib is added, is there any trial (such as NCT06696183, which allows one prior cycle but no prior gilteritinib) worth asking about, knowing that starting gilteritinib outside a trial would rule it out?

NCT06696183 NCT06191263 NCT07813390 NCT06366789 Trials

Re-reading the trials with prior-venetoclax and FLT3-inhibitor wording

On Oct 31 Claude re-read the 34 enrolling trials whose criteria mention prior venetoclax or a prior FLT3 inhibitor, using the new answers (azacitidine, first cycle, gilteritinib planned). Result: 23 blockers and 11 depends. Five verdicts changed. NCT02781883 (BP1001 with venetoclax and decitabine) and NCT03661307 (quizartinib, decitabine and venetoclax) moved from blocker to depends: both turn on whether a patient who has started venetoclax and azacitidine can still enter, and neither has a site near Seattle. NCT07407660 (shortened venetoclax guided by the day-14 marrow, ages up to 100, not yet recruiting, no site near Seattle) moved from no blocker to depends: it asks whether her ELN 2022 risk assessment was done and whether her first cycle used 12 to 14 days of venetoclax. NCT05586074 and NCT06191263 moved from depends to blocker, because both need relapsed or refractory disease. NCT06696183 (gilteritinib with venetoclax and azacitidine) stays depends: it wants exactly one completed cycle and no prior gilteritinib, so its window is the end of her first cycle. These verdicts now track her cycle count and FLT3-inhibitor answer, so updating either re-reads them automatically. NCT04588922 failed a quote check on Oct 31; the Nov 1 retry read it as depends (its Group 5 requires exactly two cycles). Question for her oncologist: does NCT07407660 or NCT06696183 fit the timing of her first cycle, and does her venetoclax schedule match what they require?

NCT07407660 NCT06696183 NCT02781883 NCT03661307 Review

Trials that accept patients already on venetoclax and azacitidine

NCT06696183 adds gilteritinib, a FLT3 inhibitor, to venetoclax and azacitidine, and allows one prior cycle of venetoclax and azacitidine. Her FLT3 D835Y and ITD make this the closest match, though it has no site near Seattle. NCT04588922 (SLS009, a CDK9 inhibitor) has a group (Group 5) for patients who have had exactly two cycles of venetoclax and azacitidine without a remission; it has no site near Seattle. NCT07407660 (phase 3) tests a shorter venetoclax course guided by the day-14 marrow, for patients on azacitidine and venetoclax. NCT06511882 studies stopping the drugs after a deep remission, which may matter later. She is in her first cycle (Oct 31). NCT06206174 (TGRX-814, in China) excludes prior BCL-2 inhibitors unless they were stopped for intolerance. NCT06696183 also excludes prior gilteritinib. Question for her oncologist: are any of these worth considering before her cycle count or a gilteritinib start puts them out of reach? Claude's reading of the criteria is a starting point, not a ruling.

NCT06696183 NCT04588922 NCT07407660 NCT06511882 NCT06206174

Latest changes: quiet trial news, and the studies on stopping venetoclax

No trial changes from Oct 30 to Nov 1, and no new papers in the last two runs. CULMINATE (NCT04023526, cusatuzumab with azacitidine) is no longer recruiting. A new paper reports prolonged treatment-free remission after venetoclax with a hypomethylating agent, but only in AML with inv(16), and her karyotype is normal (PMID 42963459). Studies that test stopping or shortening treatment and are already in the watch: NCT06511882 (stopping HMA and venetoclax once residual disease is negative), NCT07437950 (MyeloMATCH treatment lengths for older patients) and NCT07407660 (shortened venetoclax based on day-14 marrow blasts in elderly patients). Earlier this week Claude ruled out three new studies (NCT07905027, NCT06577441, NCT05334069) on age or prior-therapy limits. Recent papers close to her situation: QuANTUM-First quizartinib in patients 60 and older (PMID 42952446), and treatment-visit time on venetoclax with an injected hypomethylating agent (PMID 42952121). Question for her oncologist: if she reaches remission, how will they decide how long she stays on venetoclax and azacitidine, and would a stopping study such as NCT06511882 ever apply?

NCT06511882 NCT07437950 NCT07407660 PMID 42963459 PMID 42952446 PMID 42952121 Papers

Drug evidence on her variants (CIViC)

CIViC, an expert-curated database, has 34 accepted evidence entries for her FLT3 variants and none yet for DDX41 R525H or DNMT3A R882H. Most FLT3-ITD entries are prognostic (FLT3-ITD is linked with a poorer outcome), and the strongest treatment entry (level A) is gilteritinib in relapsed or refractory FLT3-mutated AML from the ADMIRAL trial. For D835Y, CIViC lists only laboratory evidence (level D): cells with D835Y stay sensitive to crenolanib and midostaurin but not to quizartinib or sorafenib. That matters for quizartinib trials such as NCT07478991 (VENP-A-QUI), though laboratory findings do not settle what happens in patients. Question for her oncologist: with both FLT3-ITD and D835Y, is a quizartinib-based trial less suitable for her than a gilteritinib-based one?

CIViC table NCT07478991 ADMIRAL (PMID 31665578)

A Fred Hutch study for less fit adults that the watch was missing

NCT07094750 at Fred Hutch and the University of Washington randomizes less fit adults with AML or another high-grade myeloid neoplasm between lower- and higher-intensity treatment. It has no upper age limit; patients 75 or older need ECOG 0 to 2 and at least one 'unfitness' criterion such as heart or lung limits. It started enrolling on 2026-10-13. The watch missed it because ClinicalTrials.gov files it as phase "not applicable", and that search is now part of the watch. Claude read its criteria on Nov 1 and marked it depends: it turns on her ECOG and on whether a patient who has started venetoclax and azacitidine can still be randomized. Question for her oncologist: is a treatment-strategy study like this one open to someone who has already started venetoclax, and is it worth asking the Fred Hutch team?

NCT07094750 Research log

Earlier this week: posted results on her drugs, and a later-line option

NCT04161885, a closed trial of venetoclax and azacitidine as maintenance after a transplant, has posted results. A second-opinion reading moved NCT04375631 at Fred Hutch (CLAG-M or FLAG-Ida chemotherapy, then a reduced-intensity transplant) from blocker to depends. It is for AML that has not responded to at least 2 cycles of venetoclax combinations, or has relapsed, and accepts patients 75 and older only with ECOG 0 to 1 and transplant-conference approval. It does not apply now, but it is one of the few later-line studies near Seattle without a hard age cap. Questions for her oncologist: do the NCT04161885 results say anything useful about venetoclax and azacitidine as longer-term treatment, and if her disease does not respond, is intensive salvage and a transplant ever realistic for her?

NCT04161885 results NCT04375631 Results posted

Preprints are now tracked

From today the watch also searches preprints (medRxiv, bioRxiv, Research Square and others), which can show results weeks or months before a journal prints them. The first search found 6 from the last 30 days; they are listed under Recent changes, marked as not peer reviewed. Most on point for her: an AI model that predicts response to venetoclax and azacitidine in newly diagnosed AML (PPR1342224, medRxiv). Preprints have not been checked by other scientists, so treat them as early signals to ask about, not findings.

PPR1342224 Recent changes

Second opinion: trials that may matter later, and two gaps in her record

A stronger model re-read the 96 blockers that matter most (near Seattle or ranked high) and agreed on 77 of the 91 it completed. Where it disagreed it was more cautious; a family member went through the 14 disagreements on 2026-10-26 and sided with it on 6 (next item). Two points stand out. First, several trials are for situations she may reach later: remission with residual disease, or relapse. Second, IDH-directed trials (NCT02074839, NCT04603001) were ruled out on the assumption that her panel tested IDH1 and IDH2 and found neither; her profile does not say so. NCT04603001 is no longer recruiting and also excludes more than one cycle of venetoclax. Questions for her team: how will residual disease be measured after treatment, and were IDH1 and IDH2 tested?

NCT02074839 NCT04603001

Six trials the first review ruled out too quickly

A family member checked the 14 trials where the two reviews disagreed (2026-10-26) and sided with the second opinion on these 6; for the other 8 the first review's blocker stands. NCT07645469 (WT1 T cells with azacitidine, Fred Hutch) matters most: its DNMT3A rule only applies when DNMT3A is the sole evidence of residual disease, and the trial also measures FLT3-ITD residual disease, which she has. If she reaches remission with residual disease, and is HLA-A*02:01 and WT1-positive, it could fit. NCT07623187 (IPN60340 with venetoclax and azacitidine) excludes these drugs only when given for an earlier MDS; the open point is whether she still counts as newly diagnosed and whether her ECOG is 1 to 2. NCT07082452 (7-day venetoclax) has the same MDS-only wording but is for treatment-naïve patients, needs French social-security enrollment and has no US site, so it is unlikely to matter. NCT02074839 (ivosidenib, OHSU) depends on whether she has an IDH1 mutation. NCT03737955 (gemtuzumab for residual disease, Fred Hutch) accepts adults at any point in treatment and may matter later, in remission with residual disease. NCT06859424 (mismatched-donor transplant) has no age cap on its reduced-intensity arm, but a transplant at her age is unlikely. The page still shows the first review's red pill beside the second opinion for these; this note is the record of which one stands. Claude's reading of the criteria is a starting point, not a ruling.

NCT07645469 NCT07623187 NCT07082452 NCT02074839 NCT03737955 NCT06859424

Azacitidine is in shortage

The FDA lists 4 azacitidine injection presentations as unavailable (Teva, Hikma, Fresenius Kabi, Eugia) and 1 as limited (Armas). Another 4 are marked available. If she is on azacitidine, ask the infusion center whether her supply or schedule is affected.

FDA drug shortages

The FLT3 trial at Swedish First Hill: considered, did not qualify

NCT06317649 compares azacitidine and venetoclax with and without gilteritinib in older or unfit adults with FLT3-mutated AML, at Swedish First Hill, Fred Hutch and UW. Its criteria require no prior AML therapy, and her oncologist confirmed on Oct 31 that she was considered and did not qualify. The same holds for its MyeloMATCH screening study, NCT05564390. Question for her oncologist: if her disease needs a change of treatment later, would MyeloMATCH screening be worth repeating for its relapsed-disease studies?

NCT06317649 MyeloMATCH screening NCT05564390

The top-ranked menin-inhibitor trials probably do not fit

Three of the five trials that pass every check (ziftomenib NCT07007312, revumenib NCT06652438, bleximenib NCT06852222) enroll only NPM1-mutated or KMT2A-rearranged AML, and they want patients who have not yet been treated. Her panel lists neither change, and she has started treatment. They rank high because they use venetoclax and azacitidine and have local sites. Question: was she tested for NPM1 and KMT2A? If both are negative, these can be set aside.

NCT07007312 NCT06652438 NCT06852222

DDX41: conflicting classification and a family study

ClinVar lists DDX41 R525H as conflicting (labs disagree), while the DDX41 start-codon changes that match her M1? are classified pathogenic (on Nov 2 ClinVar firmed this to pathogenic or likely pathogenic, several labs, no conflicts). With M1? at 42%, it may be inherited. Question: should she have germline testing and see a genetic counselor? NCT07019155 studies people and families with DDX41 changes, and a new Blood paper (Gagelmann, October 2026) looks at germline DDX41 as a distinct subgroup.

NCT07019155 ClinVar R525H Gagelmann 2026

Near Seattle Seattle Children's Hospital, Seattle (0 mi); Oregon Health and Science University, Portland (145 mi)

Matched setting: relapsed

Summary and interventions

Biospecimen Collection, Clinical Evaluation, Neurocognitive Assessment, Questionnaire Administration, Survey Administration

This study attempts to learn more about the health of persons with Down syndrome after treatment for acute leukemia. Children with Down syndrome are at increased risk for side effects during treatment for acute leukemia, but it is unclear of their risk for long-term effects of cancer treatment. By learning more about the factors that may contribute to chronic health conditions and long-term effects after treatment for leukemia in persons with Down syndrome, clinical practice guidelines for survivorship care can be developed to help improve their quality-of-life.

NCT07094750Strategy and care study (phase not applicable)

Randomization for the Identification of Best Treatment Intensity for Less Fit Adults With Acute Myeloid Leukemia and Myeloid Neoplasms

Interventional · Recruiting · Ages 18 to no limit · score 12

  • Age 84
  • Myeloid
  • Enrolling
  • Non-intensive
  • No transplant
  • Near Seattle
  • Phase 3/4
Depends on: ECOG performance status Reviewed by Claude 2026-11-01
Quoted criterion, inclusion

Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group (ECOG) performance status of 0 - 3 (for patients aged < 75 years) or ECOG performance status of 0 - 2 (for patients aged ≥ 75 years)

Question for her team: What is her ECOG performance status? Also, does having already started azacitidine plus venetoclax affect whether she could join this treatment-intensity randomization?

Near Seattle Fred Hutch/University of Washington Cancer Consortium, Seattle (0 mi)

Matched drugs: venetoclax · population: unfit, older patients, non-intensive · supportive: supportive care, palliative, fatigue, infection

Summary and interventions

Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Collection, Electronic Health Record Review, Questionnaire Administration

This clinical trial studies whether less fit adults with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) or myeloid neoplasms are willing to let a computer program decide (randomization) whether they receive lower- or higher-intensity chemotherapy. Historically, treatment decision-making for patients with AML or myeloid neoplasms has divided patients into two categories, with patients considered fit receiving intensive "curative" chemotherapy, and patients considered unfit, such as older patients with a higher risk of early death from therapy, receiving non-intensive "palliative" therapy or no therapy. With the introduction of new treatment agents, it has become difficult to determine the difference between intensive and non-intensive therapy, especially for patients considered unfit for whom treatment-related side effects remain a concern. Treatment intensity is best identified through randomized trials but often patients are unwilling to undergo randomization due to preset beliefs. However, with improved supportive care and the awareness that new treatment agents may have similar risks as intensive therapy, it may be possible that more patients are willing to be randomized. Summary and interventions

Luspatercept Injection [Reblozyl]

Myelodysplastic syndromes, primarily affecting older adults, are a heterogeneous group of clonal disorders of hematopoietic stem cells characterized by ineffective hematopoiesis that manifest clinically as anemia, neutropenia, and/or thrombocytopenia of variable severity; these often result in RBC- transfusion dependent (TD) anemia, increased risk of infection, and/or hemorrhage, as well as a potential to progress to acute myeloid leukemia (AML). Lenalidomide is approved for red blood cell transfusion-dependent (RBC TD) anemia due to low-risk myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) with a chromosome 5q deletion (del5q) with or without additional cytogenetic abnormalities. About one third of patients are refractory/resistant/intolerant and will require further treatment options. Luspatercept (ACE-536), an erythroid maturation agent, is a recombinant fusion protein consisting of a modified form of the extracellular domain (ECD) of the human activin receptor type IIB (ActRIIB) linked to the Fc portion of human immunoglobulin G1 (IgG1-Fc). TAK-226

Summary and interventions

Dapagliflozin (10mg Tab)

This study is a prospective, single-arm, phase II clinical trial designed to evaluate the efficacy and safety of dapagliflozin in improving anemia in patients with lower-risk myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS). Anemia is the most common clinical problem in patients with lower-risk MDS and often leads to fatigue, reduced quality of life, and the need for repeated blood transfusions. Current treatment options, including erythropoiesis-stimulating agents and other therapies, are not effective in all patients, and additional treatment options are needed. Dapagliflozin is a sodium-glucose cotransporter-2 (SGLT2) inhibitor that is widely used for the treatment of diabetes, heart failure, and chronic kidney disease. Previous studies have shown that SGLT2 inhibitors can increase hemoglobin levels, possibly by stimulating erythropoiesis. In this study, eligible patients will receive dapagliflozin 10 mg orally once daily for 24 weeks. The primary objective is to evaluate the hemoglobin response rate during the study period. Secondary objectives include changes in hemoglobin levels, transfusion requirements, and safety outcomes. This study aims to explore whether dapagliflozin can serve as a

  • Myeloid
  • Enrolling
  • Non-intensive
  • No transplant
  • Near Seattle
  • Phase 3/4
Blocking criterion Reviewed by Claude 2026-11-01
Quoted criterion, exclusion

Concurrent diagnosis of acute myeloid leukemia (AML) or other active hematologic malignancy.

Question for her team: Given her AML diagnosis and current azacitidine, does this MDS trial's exclusion of concurrent AML apply to her?

Matched population: older patients · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Chidamide

Myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) are a group of bone marrow disorders that can cause low blood cell counts and may progress to acute leukemia. Treatment options for patients with intermediate-to-high-risk MDS are limited, especially for older patients or those who are not suitable for intensive chemotherapy or hypomethylating agents. Chidamide is an oral histone deacetylase inhibitor that has shown antitumor activity in several hematologic malignancies. This study aims to evaluate the effectiveness and safety of chidamide used alone in patients with intermediate-to-high-risk MDS. This is a prospective, single-arm, open-label phase II study conducted at a single center. Eligible participants will receive oral chidamide twice weekly and will be followed for treatment response and side effects. The results of this study may help determine whether chidamide could be a potential treatment option for patients with intermediate-to-high-risk MDS who have limited therapeutic choices.

Summary and interventions

Preemptive DLI, Prophylactic DLI

Donor lymphocyte infusion (DLI) based on minimal residual disease (MRD) has been widely adopted worldwide to enhance the graft-versus-leukemia effect following allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (allo-HSCT). However, there is a lack of studies comparing the efficacy and safety of prophylactic versus preemptive DLI in patients with refractory/relapsed (R/R) acute myeloid leukemia (AML), and its effectiveness in other myeloid neoplasms (MN, such as myelodysplastic syndromes with excess blasts, MDS-IB) remains unknown, particularly with a scarcity of data from randomized controlled trials. This multicenter, randomized controlled study aims to prospectively compare the efficacy and safety of prophylactic versus preemptive DLI in patients with R/R myeloid neoplasms undergoing allo-HSCT. The study will enroll patients with MN (including AML and MDS-IB, excluding Ph+ cases) undergoing allo-HSCT, who are in R/R status at the time of transplant and achieve MRD-negative remission at 1 month post-transplant. Matched drugs: azacitidine, decitabine · setting: relapsed, refractory · supportive: symptom

Summary and interventions

Axatilimab, Azacitidine, Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration and Biopsy, Survey Administration

This phase Ib/II trial tests the best dose of axatilimab and effectiveness of axatilimab with or without azacitidine for the treatment of patients with advanced phase myeloproliferative neoplasms (MPN), myeloproliferative neoplasm/myelodysplastic syndrome (MPN/MDS) overlap or high risk chronic myelomonocytic leukemia (CMML). Axatilimab is an antibody that is cloned from a single white blood cell that is known to be able to recognize cancer cells and block a protein on the surface of the white blood cells that may be involved in cancer cell growth. By blocking the proteins, this may slow or halt the growth of the cancer. Azacitidine is in a class of medications called antimetabolites. It works by stopping or slowing the growth of cancer cells. Giving axatilimab with or without azacitidine may be safe and effective in treating patients with advanced phase MPN, MPN/MDS overlap or high risk CMML.

Matched genes: FLT3 · drugs: venetoclax · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

ATG dosing platform when combined with standard tacrolimus and mini methotrexate

In an effort to reduce graft versus host disease (GVHD) and enhance graft versus leukemia (GVL) effect post allogenic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (AHSCT), recent research has focused on host immune cell depletion. Frame shifting anti-thymocyte globulin (ATG) backwards to earlier days before days 0 can result in deeper host and less graft T-cell depletion, leading to better immune reconstitution. Preliminary data where 80% of the ATG dose is given on days -6,-5,-4 and 20% given on day -1, showed effective prevention of severe acute GVHD, chronic GVHD and favorable early immune reconstitution. We hypothesize that our 2 step ATG dosing platform when combined with standard tacrolimus and mini methotrexate can prevent grade III-IV acute GVHD and chronic GVHD, resulting in improvement of GVHD/relapse free survival at one year post transplant.

Summary and interventions

Hematopoietic stem-cell transplantation

Three recent prospective "transplant/no transplant" studies concluded to an advantage of OS with transplantation in patients with high or intermediate-2 IPSS risk (not significant in Kröger's study). No prospective randomized trial has assessed the pre-transplant therapy in MDS patients yet but some information can be extracted from these 3 recent studies. In the French study (n=162), 72% patients with a donor received HSCT, previously treated by hypomethylating agent (HMA) in 71% of them. There was a trend to a better survival in patients achieving a complete remission with pre-graft therapy (HR: 0.55, p=0.088) and higher risk of death in unresponsiveness patients transformed into AML (HR: 2.36, p=0.008). In Nakamura's study (n=384), 83% of patients with a donor were transplanted, previously treated by HMA in 68%2. The multivariable Cox model for Overall Survival (OS) and Leukemia-free survival showed an excess risk in patients treated by HMA. Moreover, responders still have a higher risk of mortality as compared to patients who did not receive any pre-graft therapy (HR: 2.417, p=0.0054). Matched drugs: gilteritinib, venetoclax, decitabine · setting: maintenance, measurable residual disease · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Decitabine and Cedazuridine, Enasidenib, Gilteritinib, Ivosidenib, Venetoclax

This phase Ib trial is to find out the side effects and possible benefits of decitabine alone or given together with venetoclax, gilteritinib, enasidenib, or ivosidenib in treating patients with acute myeloid leukemia that is under control (remission). Chemotherapy drugs, such as decitabine, work in different ways to stop the growth of cancer cells, either by killing the cells, by stopping them from dividing, or by stopping them from spreading. Venetoclax may stop the growth of cancer cells by blocking a protein called Bcl-2 needed for cell growth. Gilteritinib, enasidenib, and ivosidenib may stop the growth of cancer cells by blocking some of the enzymes needed for cell growth. Giving decitabine alone or together with venetoclax, gilteritinib, enasidenib, or ivosidenib may help to control the disease.

Found by a text pattern, not reviewed. Check the full criteria on ClinicalTrials.gov.

Near Seattle Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-First Hill, Seattle (0 mi); University of Washington Medical Center - Montlake, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Edmonds, Edmonds (14 mi)

Matched drugs: gilteritinib, quizartinib, venetoclax, azacitidine, decitabine

Summary and interventions

Allogeneic Hematopoietic Stem Cell Transplantation, Azacitidine, Best Practice, Biopsy Procedure, Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Busulfan, Chest Radiography, Computed Tomography, Cytarabine, Daunorubicin

This MyeloMATCH Master Screening and Reassessment Protocol (MSRP) evaluates the use of a screening tool and specific laboratory tests to help improve participants' ability to register to clinical trials throughout the course of their myeloid cancer (acute myeloid leukemia or myelodysplastic syndrome) treatment. This study involves testing patients' bone marrow and blood for certain biomarkers. A biomarker (sometimes called a marker) is any molecule in the body that can be measured. Doctors look at markers to learn what is happening in the body. Knowing about certain markers can give doctors more information about what is driving the cancer and how to treat it. Testing patients' bone marrow and blood will show doctors if patients have markers that specific drugs can target. The marker testing in this study will let doctors know if they can match patients with a treatment study (myeloMATCH clinical trial) that tests treatment for the type of cancer they have or continue standard of care treatment with their doctor on the Tier Advancement Pathway (TAP).

This phase II MyeloMATCH treatment trial compares ASTX727 with standard duration versus shorter duration of venetoclax for the treatment of newly diagnosed acute myeloid leukemia (AML). ASTX727 is a combination of decitabine and cedazuridine. Decitabine is in a class of medications called hypomethylation agents. It works by helping the bone marrow produce normal blood cells and by killing abnormal cells in the bone marrow. Cedazuridine is in a class of medications called cytidine deaminase inhibitors. It prevents the breakdown of decitabine, making it more available in the body so that decitabine will have a greater effect. Venetoclax is in a class of medications called B-cell lymphoma-2 (BCL-2) inhibitors. It may stop the growth of cancer cells by blocking Bcl-2, a protein needed for cancer cell survival. Shorter duration venetoclax may be as effective as standard duration venetoclax when given with ASTX727 for the treatment of newly diagnosed AML.

Web based survey

NCT04267848At her center

Testing the Addition of a Type of Drug Called Immunotherapy to the Usual Chemotherapy Treatment for Non-small Cell Lung Cancer, an ALCHEMIST Treatment Trial (Chemo-IO [ACCIO])

Phase 3 · Recruiting · Ages 18 to no limit · score 1

Changed: last_update

  • Age 84
  • Myeloid
  • Enrolling
  • Non-intensive
  • No transplant
  • Near Seattle
  • Phase 3/4

Near Seattle Pacific Gynecology Specialists, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-Ballard Campus, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-Cherry Hill, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-First Hill, Seattle (0 mi)

Matched supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Biospecimen Collection, Carboplatin, Cisplatin, Computed Tomography, Echocardiography Test, Gemcitabine Hydrochloride, Magnetic Resonance Imaging, Observation Activity, Paclitaxel, Pembrolizumab, Pemetrexed Disodium, Questionnaire Administration

This phase III ALCHEMIST treatment trial tests the addition of pembrolizumab to usual chemotherapy for the treatment of stage IIA, IIB, IIIA or IIIB non-small cell lung cancer that has been removed by surgery. Immunotherapy with monoclonal antibodies, such as pembrolizumab, may help the body's immune system attack the cancer, and may interfere with the ability of tumor cells to grow and spread. Chemotherapy drugs, such as cisplatin, pemetrexed, carboplatin, gemcitabine hydrochloride, and paclitaxel, work in different ways to stop the growth of tumor cells, either by killing the cells, by stopping them from dividing, or by stopping them from spreading. Giving pembrolizumab with usual chemotherapy may help increase survival times in patients with stage IIA, IIB, IIIA or IIIB non-small cell lung cancer.

MESI-HM: Middle East Study of Infections in Patients With Hematological Malignancies

Summary and interventions

No Intervention: Observational Cohort

The goal of this prospective observational study is to is to prospectively study the epidemiology of bloodstream infection (BSI) in patients with hematological malignancies (HM) and hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT) recipients across the Middle East. The objectives are to describe the epidemiological trends, identify risk factors for inappropriate antimicrobial therapy, and predictors of persistent BSI, microbiological relapse, and all-cause mortality. The study is entirely observational, participants will receive standard clinical care as directed by their treating doctors.

Testing the Addition of Venetoclax or Gemtuzumab Ozogamicin (GO) to Usual Treatment Regimen (Cytarabine and Daunorubicin, "7+3") for Core Binding Factor Acute Myeloid Leukemia (CBF-AML) to Improve Response (A MYELOMATCH Treatment Trial)

Phase 2 · Recruiting · Ages 18 to 59 · score 6

Changed: last_update

  • Age 84
  • Myeloid
  • Enrolling
  • Non-intensive
  • No transplant
  • Near Seattle
  • Phase 3/4
Blocking criterion Reviewed by Claude 2026-10-26
Quoted criterion, inclusion

Age 18-59 years

Question for her team: This trial requires age 18-59 and core binding factor AML, while she is 84 with a normal karyotype; does that rule it out?

Second opinion (Opus 5 5): agrees.

Near Seattle Swedish Medical Center-First Hill, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Edmonds, Edmonds (14 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Issaquah, Issaquah (15 mi)

Matched genes: FLT3 · drugs: venetoclax · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Cytarabine, Daunorubicin Hydrochloride, Echocardiography Test, Gemtuzumab Ozogamicin, Multigated Acquisition Scan, Venetoclax

This phase II MYELOMATCH treatment trial compares the effect of venetoclax to gemtuzumab ozogamicin, when given with cytarabine and daunorubicin ("7+3" regimen), for the treatment of patients with core binding factor acute myeloid leukemia (CBF-AML). Venetoclax is in a class of medications called B-cell lymphoma-2 (BCL-2) inhibitors. It may stop the growth of cancer cells by blocking Bcl-2, a protein needed for cancer cell survival. Gemtuzumab ozogamicin is a monoclonal antibody, called gemtuzumab, linked to an antitumor antibiotic drug, called ozogamicin. Gemtuzumab is a form of targeted therapy because it attaches to specific molecules (receptors) on the surface of cancer cells, known as CD33 receptors, and delivers ozogamicin to kill them. Chemotherapy drugs, such as cytarabine and daunorubicin work in different ways to stop the growth of cancer cells either by killing the cells, by stopping them from dividing, or by stopping them from spreading. Giving venetoclax with cytarabine and daunorubicin may have fewer side effects and be as effective or better than the combination with gemtuzumab ozogamicin in treating patients with core binding factor AML.

NCT06295367At her center

Cost Communication and Financial Navigation in Cancer Patients (COSTCOM)

Interventional · Recruiting · score 3

  • Age 84
  • Myeloid
  • Enrolling
  • Non-intensive
  • No transplant
  • Near Seattle
  • Phase 3/4

Near Seattle Swedish Medical Center-Ballard Campus, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-Cherry Hill, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-First Hill, Seattle (0 mi); Valley Medical Center, Renton (10 mi)

Matched supportive: quality of life, palliative, caregiver

Summary and interventions

Best Practice, Financial Navigation, Interview, Survey Administration

This clinical trial evaluates the effect of Cost Communication and Financial Navigation (CostCOM) intervention on adherence to care and financial burden in cancer patients. Many cancer patients experience financial hardship due to high medical out of pocket costs (OOPC), changes in employment, income and insurance. Financial hardship can lead to a delay or a stop in cancer care, and is linked to poor quality of life. Financial navigation programs, such as CostCOM, provide financial counseling, education and connections to appropriate resources to reduce financial barriers to healthcare and minimize financial stress and burden. CostCOM may improve adherence to care and decrease financial burden in patients with cancer.

Found by a text pattern, not reviewed. Check the full criteria on ClinicalTrials.gov.

Near Seattle Seattle Children's Hospital, Seattle (0 mi)

Matched setting: relapsed, refractory · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Biological SC-CAR.CLEC2Av1

This is a phase I, open-label, non-randomized clinical trial that will enroll pediatric and adult research participants with CLEC2A+ acute myeloid leukemia (AML) that does not go away with treatment (is refractory) or comes back after treatment (is recurrent). The goal is to evaluate the safety, feasibility, and efficacy of administering T cell products (modified immune cells) derived from the research participant's blood that have been genetically modified to express chimeric antigen receptor (CAR) targeting SC-CAR.CLEC2Av1. The main questions it seeks to answer are: * How safe is the T cell product infusion (modified immune cells)? * How feasible is it to manufacture the cell products? Participants will first receive treatments to prepare their body (bridging therapy and lymphodepletion). On Day 0, they receive a single infusion of CLEC2A CAR T cells, which are modified immune cells designed to target and fight cancer. Researchers will evaluate the safety and side effects of the treatment, check how well the CAR T cells survive and multiply in the body over time (using blood, bone marrow, and cerebrospinal fluid samples), and assess how well the treatment fights the AML. Near Seattle Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-First Hill, Seattle (0 mi); University of Washington Medical Center - Montlake, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Edmonds, Edmonds (14 mi)

Matched drugs: gilteritinib, quizartinib, venetoclax, azacitidine, decitabine

Summary and interventions

Allogeneic Hematopoietic Stem Cell Transplantation, Azacitidine, Best Practice, Biopsy Procedure, Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Busulfan, Chest Radiography, Computed Tomography, Cytarabine, Daunorubicin

This MyeloMATCH Master Screening and Reassessment Protocol (MSRP) evaluates the use of a screening tool and specific laboratory tests to help improve participants' ability to register to clinical trials throughout the course of their myeloid cancer (acute myeloid leukemia or myelodysplastic syndrome) treatment. This study involves testing patients' bone marrow and blood for certain biomarkers. A biomarker (sometimes called a marker) is any molecule in the body that can be measured. Doctors look at markers to learn what is happening in the body. Knowing about certain markers can give doctors more information about what is driving the cancer and how to treat it. Testing patients' bone marrow and blood will show doctors if patients have markers that specific drugs can target. The marker testing in this study will let doctors know if they can match patients with a treatment study (myeloMATCH clinical trial) that tests treatment for the type of cancer they have or continue standard of care treatment with their doctor on the Tier Advancement Pathway (TAP).

NCT07616921Supportive care study

A Home-Based Exercise Intervention (CAREFit-BMT) in Improving Heart Function Among Patients With High Risk Acute Myeloid Leukemia Undergoing Stem Cell Transplant

Interventional · Recruiting · Ages 18 to no limit · score 3

Changed: last_update

  • Age 84
  • Myeloid
  • Enrolling
  • Non-intensive
  • No transplant
  • Near Seattle
  • Phase 3/4
Blocking criterion Reviewed by Claude 2026-10-26
Quoted criterion, inclusion

New diagnosis of acute myeloid leukemia at Fred Hutch

Question for her team: This trial requires a new AML diagnosis at Fred Hutch; is she being diagnosed or treated there, given she has already started HMA-venetoclax?

Second opinion (Opus 5 5) disagrees Depends on: other information. Quoted: New diagnosis of acute myeloid leukemia at Fred Hutch Worth raising with her team.

Near Seattle Fred Hutch/University of Washington Cancer Consortium, Seattle (0 mi)

Matched population: older adults · supportive: exercise

Summary and interventions

Aerobic Exercise, Biospecimen Collection, Electronic Health Record Review, Interview, Resistance Training, Survey Administration

This clinical trial studies how well a home-based exercise intervention called Cardiorespiratory Fitness in Bone Marrow Transplant Program (CAREFit-BMT) works in improving heart function among patients with high risk acute myeloid leukemia (AML) undergoing stem cell transplant. Older adults and those with other medical conditions are at a higher risk for complications in the heart and blood vessels (cardiovascular). Older age at transplantation has been associated with nearly all cardiovascular complications occurring after stem cell transplant. This is likely explained by the structural and functional changes that occur in aging hearts as well as the larger burden of cardiovascular risk factors such as diabetes, high blood pressure, and obesity. CAREFit-BMT is a "prehabilitation" program, or exercise initiated prior to intensive therapy, that consists of aerobic exercises and strength training. CAREFit-BMT may be able to increase the likelihood of safe and successful transition to stem cell transplant in patients with high risk AML.

The Cancer Experience Registry®: An Online Survey Research Study to Understand the Experiences of Those Impacted By a Cancer Diagnosis. Autologous HLA-A*02:01-restricted CD4+/CD8+ Anti-WT1 TCR/CD8ab-expressing T-cells, Azacitidine, Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Chest Radiography, Computed Tomography, Echocardiography Test, Lumbar Puncture, Multigated Acquisition Scan, Positron Emission Tomography

This phase I trial tests the safety, side effects and best dose of FH-WT1-E50 TCR T cells with azacitidine for the treatment of minimal residual disease (MRD) positive acute myeloid leukemia (AML). T cells are infection fighting blood cells that can kill tumor cells. The T cells given in this study will come from the patient and will have a new gene put in them that makes them able to recognize WT1, a protein on the surface of cancer cells. These WT1-specific T cells may help the body's immune system identify and kill WT1 cancer cells. Azacitidine is in a class of medications called antimetabolites. It works by stopping or slowing the growth of cancer cells. Giving FH-WT1-E50 TCR T Cells with azacitidine may be safe and/or effective for the treatment of MRD positive AML.

Summary and interventions

Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Decitabine and Cedazuridine, Enasidenib

This phase II MyeloMATCH treatment trial compares the usual treatment of cedazuridine-decitabine (ASTX727) to the combination treatment of ASTX727 and enasidenib in treating patients with higher-risk, IDH2-mutated myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS). ASTX727 is a combination of two drugs, decitabine and cedazuridine. Cedazuridine is in a class of medications called cytidine deaminase inhibitors. It prevents the breakdown of decitabine, making it more available in the body so that decitabine will have a greater effect. Decitabine is in a class of medications called hypomethylation agents. It works by helping the bone marrow produce normal blood cells and by killing abnormal cells in the bone marrow. Enasidenib is an enzyme inhibitor that may stop the growth of cells by blocking some of the enzymes needed for cell growth. Giving ASTX727 in combination with enasidenib may be effective in treating patients with higher-risk IDH2-mutated MDS.

Summary and interventions

Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Computed Tomography, Obinutuzumab, Questionnaire Administration, Venetoclax

This phase III trial compares early treatment with venetoclax and obinutuzumab versus delayed treatment with venetoclax and obinutuzumab in patients with newly diagnosed high-risk chronic lymphocytic leukemia or small lymphocytic lymphoma. Venetoclax is in a class of medications called B-cell lymphoma-2 (BCL-2) inhibitors. It may stop the growth of cancer cells by blocking Bcl-2, a protein needed for cancer cell survival. Immunotherapy with monoclonal antibodies, such as obinutuzumab, may help the body's immune system attack the cancer, and may interfere with the ability of tumor cells to grow and spread. Starting treatment with the venetoclax and obinutuzumab early (before patients have symptoms) may have better outcomes for patients with chronic lymphocytic leukemia or small lymphocytic lymphoma compared to starting treatment with the venetoclax and obinutuzumab after patients show symptoms.

This phase III trial compares the effect of bevacizumab and osimertinib combination vs. osimertinib alone for the treatment of non-small cell lung cancer that has spread outside of the lungs (stage IIIB-IV) and has a change (mutation) in a gene called EGFR. The EGFR protein is involved in cell signaling pathways that control cell division and survival. Osimertinib may stop the growth of tumor cells by blocking EGFR that is needed for cell growth in this type of cancer. Bevacizumab is in a class of medications called antiangiogenic agents. Giving osimertinib with bevacizumab may control cancer for longer and help patients live longer as compared to osimertinib alone.

  • Age 84
  • Myeloid
  • Enrolling
  • Non-intensive
  • No transplant
  • Near Seattle
  • Phase 3/4

Near Seattle FHCC at Northwest Hospital, Seattle (0 mi); Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-Ballard Campus, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-Cherry Hill, Seattle (0 mi)

Summary and interventions

Screening Platform

This screening and multi-sub-study randomized phase II/III trial will establish a method for genomic screening of similar large cancer populations followed by assigning and accruing simultaneously to a multi-sub-study hybrid Master Protocol (Lung-MAP). The type of cancer trait (biomarker) will determine to which sub-study, within this protocol, a participant will be assigned to compare new targeted cancer therapy, designed to block the growth and spread of cancer, or combinations to standard of care therapy with the ultimate goal of being able to approve new targeted therapies in this setting. In addition, the protocol includes non-match sub-studies which will include all screened patients not eligible for any of the biomarker-driven sub-studies.

NCT03150693At her center

Inotuzumab Ozogamicin and Frontline Chemotherapy in Treating Young Adults With Newly Diagnosed B Acute Lymphoblastic Leukemia

Phase 3 · Recruiting · Ages 18 to 39 · score 1

  • Age 84
  • Myeloid
  • Enrolling
  • Non-intensive
  • No transplant
  • Near Seattle
  • Phase 3/4

Near Seattle Kaiser Permanente Washington, Seattle (0 mi); Pacific Gynecology Specialists, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-Ballard Campus, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-Cherry Hill, Seattle (0 mi)

Matched supportive: transfusion

Summary and interventions

Allopurinol, Bone Marrow Aspiration and Biopsy, Cyclophosphamide, Cytarabine, Daunorubicin Hydrochloride, Dexamethasone, Doxorubicin, Inotuzumab Ozogamicin, Laboratory Biomarker Analysis, Mercaptopurine, Methotrexate, Pegylated L-Asparaginase

This phase III trial studies the side effects of inotuzumab ozogamicin and how well it works when given with frontline chemotherapy in treating patients with newly diagnosed B acute lymphoblastic leukemia. Inotuzumab ozogamicin is a monoclonal antibody, called inotuzumab, linked to a chemotherapy drug called ozogamicin. Inotuzumab is a form of targeted therapy because it attaches to specific molecules (receptors) on the surface of cancer cells, known as CD22 receptors, and delivers ozogamicin to kill them. Chemotherapy drugs, such as \[intervention\], work in different ways to stop the growth of cancer cells, either by killing the cells, by stopping them from dividing, or by stopping them from spreading. Giving inotuzumab ozogamicin with chemotherapy may work better in treating young adults with B acute lymphoblastic leukemia.

NCT06724237At her center

Testing Whether High Dose Chemotherapy and Infusion of the Patients' Own Stem Cells Improves Survival in Patients With Peripheral T-cell Lymphoma Who Achieved a Complete Response at the End of the Initial Chemotherapy

Phase 3 · Recruiting · Ages 18 to 75 · score 1

  • Age 84
  • Myeloid
  • Enrolling
  • Non-intensive
  • No transplant
  • Near Seattle
  • Phase 3/4

Near Seattle Swedish Medical Center-Ballard Campus, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-Cherry Hill, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-First Hill, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Edmonds, Edmonds (14 mi)

Matched supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Autologous Hematopoietic Stem Cell Transplantation, Best Practice, Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Computed Tomography, High Dose Chemotherapy, Leukapheresis, Positron Emission Tomography, Stem Cell Mobilization Therapy

This phase III trial compares the effect of high dose chemotherapy and the patients' own (autologous) stem cells to observation only in patients with peripheral T-cell lymphoma who achieved a complete response after initial chemotherapy. Usual treatment after a complete response may include observation or high dose chemotherapy followed by an autologous stem cell transplant, however, it is not known if a transplant if beneficial. Giving chemotherapy before a stem cell transplant helps kill cancer cells in the body and helps make room in the patient's bone marrow for new blood-forming cells (stem cells) to grow. Stem cells removed prior to treatment are then returned to the patient to replace the blood forming cells that were destroyed by the chemotherapy. Giving high dose chemotherapy followed by an autologous stem cell transplant may be more effective compared to observation only in treating patients with peripheral T-cell lymphoma who have achieved a complete response after initial chemotherapy.

NCT01881334Expanded access

Summary and interventions

CliniMACS CD34 Reagent System

The objective of this study is to make T-cell depleted stem cells from a family member who is a half match (haplo-identical) available on an expanded access basis to patients receiving one or two unrelated cord blood transplants who are at a higher risk of not engrafting in a safe amount of time. The purpose of the related stem cells is to give the bone marrow a "jump start" towards recovery. Ultimately, the cord blood cells will grow and permanently rescue the bone marrow.

NCT07900542Supportive care study

MESI-HM: Middle East Study of Infections in Patients With Hematological Malignancies

Observational · Recruiting · Ages 18 to no limit · score 2

  • Age 84
  • Myeloid
  • Enrolling
  • Non-intensive
  • No transplant
  • Near Seattle
  • Phase 3/4

Matched supportive: infection, infections

Summary and interventions

No Intervention: Observational Cohort

The goal of this prospective observational study is to is to prospectively study the epidemiology of bloodstream infection (BSI) in patients with hematological malignancies and stem cell transplant recipients across the Middle East. The objectives are to describe the epidemiological trends, identify risk factors for inappropriate antimicrobial therapy, and predictors of persistent BSI, microbiological relapse, and all-cause mortality. The study is entirely observational, participants will receive standard clinical care as directed by their treating doctors.

Found by a text pattern, not reviewed. Check the full criteria on ClinicalTrials.gov.

Near Seattle Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-First Hill, Seattle (0 mi); University of Washington Medical Center - Montlake, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Edmonds, Edmonds (14 mi)

Matched genes: FLT3 · drugs: gilteritinib, venetoclax, azacitidine · population: unfit, older patients · supportive: supportive care, caregiver, infection

Summary and interventions

Azacitidine, Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Gilteritinib, Venetoclax

This phase II MyeloMATCH treatment trial compares the usual treatment of azacitidine and venetoclax to the combination treatment of azacitidine, venetoclax and gilteritinib in treating older and unfit patients with acute myeloid leukemia and FLT3 mutations. Azacitidine is a drug that is absorbed into DNA and leads to the activation of cancer suppressor genes, which are genes that help control cell growth. Venetoclax is in a class of medications called B-cell lymphoma-2 (BCL-2) inhibitors. It may stop the growth of cancer cells by blocking Bcl-2, a protein needed for cancer cell survival. Gilteritinib is in a class of medications called kinase inhibitors. It works by blocking the action of a certain naturally occurring substance that may be needed to help cancer cells multiply. This study may help doctors find out if these different approaches are better than the usual approaches. To decide if they are better, the study doctors are looking to see if the study drugs lead to a higher percentage of patients achieving a deeper remission compared to the usual approach.

Matched genes: FLT3 · drugs: quizartinib, venetoclax, azacitidine · population: unfit · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Azacitidine, Quizartinib, Venetoclax

The goal of this clinical trial is to learn if Venetoclax+Azacytidine+Quizartinib works better than standard therapy (Venetoclax+Azacytdine) to treat naïve adult patients with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) who are not suitable for standard induction therapy due to age, co-morbidities or other risk factors. The main question it aims to answer is: \- Does the combination of Venetoclax+Azacytidine+Quizartinib show more probability of overall survival than Venetoclax+Azacytdine? Researchers will compare Venetoclax+Azacytidine+Quizartinib to Venetoclax+Azacytdine to see if Venetoclax+Azacytidine+Quizartinib works better than Venetoclax+Azacytdine to treat AML. Participants will be randomized to one of the two treatment arms in a 1:1 ratio, both of which will have treatment cycles of 28 days.

Comparing Different Treatment Lengths for Venetoclax in Older People With Newly Diagnosed Acute Myeloid Leukemia (A MyeloMATCH Treatment Trial)

Possible requirement: no prior treatment for AML Her answers: prior treatment: hma_venetoclax_started
Matched text, inclusion criteria

* Participants must have newly diagnosed, untreated acute myeloid leukemia (AML) defined by

Found by a text pattern, not reviewed. Check the full criteria on ClinicalTrials.gov.

Matched genes: FLT3 · drugs: venetoclax, decitabine · population: unfit, older people · setting: MRD · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Decitabine and Cedazuridine, Venetoclax

This phase II MyeloMATCH treatment trial compares ASTX727 with standard duration versus shorter duration of venetoclax for the treatment of newly diagnosed acute myeloid leukemia (AML). ASTX727 is a combination of decitabine and cedazuridine. Decitabine is in a class of medications called hypomethylation agents. It works by helping the bone marrow produce normal blood cells and by killing abnormal cells in the bone marrow. Cedazuridine is in a class of medications called cytidine deaminase inhibitors. It prevents the breakdown of decitabine, making it more available in the body so that decitabine will have a greater effect. Venetoclax is in a class of medications called B-cell lymphoma-2 (BCL-2) inhibitors. It may stop the growth of cancer cells by blocking Bcl-2, a protein needed for cancer cell survival. Shorter duration venetoclax may be as effective as standard duration venetoclax when given with ASTX727 for the treatment of newly diagnosed AML.

Summary and interventions

Azacitidine, Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Cytarabine, Daunorubicin Hydrochloride, Echocardiography Test, Multigated Acquisition Scan, Venetoclax

This phase II MyeloMATCH treatment trial compares cytarabine with daunorubicin versus cytarabine with daunorubicin and venetoclax versus venetoclax with azacitidine for the treatment of younger patients with intermediate risk acute myeloid leukemia (AML). Cytarabine is a drug that inhibits some of the enzymes needed for deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA) replication and repair and can slow or stop the growth of cancer cells. Daunorubicin is a drug that blocks a certain enzyme needed for cell division and DNA repair, and it may kill cancer cells. Venetoclax is in a class of medications called B-cell lymphoma-2 (BCL-2) inhibitors. It may stop the growth of cancer cells by blocking Bcl-2, a protein needed for cancer cell survival. Azacitidine is a drug that interacts with DNA to activate tumor-suppressing genes, resulting in an anti-tumor effect. Adding venetoclax to cytarabine and daunorubicin, and adding venetoclax to azacitidine, may work better than the usual treatment of cytarabine with daunorubicin alone. To decide if they are better, the study doctors are looking to see if venetoclax increases the rate of elimination of AML in participants by 20% or more compared to the usual approach

Testing the Addition of Venetoclax or Gemtuzumab Ozogamicin (GO) to Usual Treatment Regimen (Cytarabine and Daunorubicin, "7+3") for Core Binding Factor Acute Myeloid Leukemia (CBF-AML) to Improve Response (A MYELOMATCH Treatment Trial)

Near Seattle Swedish Medical Center-First Hill, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Edmonds, Edmonds (14 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Issaquah, Issaquah (15 mi)

Matched genes: FLT3 · drugs: venetoclax · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Cytarabine, Daunorubicin Hydrochloride, Echocardiography Test, Gemtuzumab Ozogamicin, Multigated Acquisition Scan, Venetoclax

This phase II MYELOMATCH treatment trial compares the effect of venetoclax to gemtuzumab ozogamicin, when given with cytarabine and daunorubicin ("7+3" regimen), for the treatment of patients with core binding factor acute myeloid leukemia (CBF-AML). Venetoclax is in a class of medications called B-cell lymphoma-2 (BCL-2) inhibitors. It may stop the growth of cancer cells by blocking Bcl-2, a protein needed for cancer cell survival. Gemtuzumab ozogamicin is a monoclonal antibody, called gemtuzumab, linked to an antitumor antibiotic drug, called ozogamicin. Gemtuzumab is a form of targeted therapy because it attaches to specific molecules (receptors) on the surface of cancer cells, known as CD33 receptors, and delivers ozogamicin to kill them. Chemotherapy drugs, such as cytarabine and daunorubicin work in different ways to stop the growth of cancer cells either by killing the cells, by stopping them from dividing, or by stopping them from spreading. Giving venetoclax with cytarabine and daunorubicin may have fewer side effects and be as effective or better than the combination with gemtuzumab ozogamicin in treating patients with core binding factor AML.

Summary and interventions

Azacitidine, Best Supportive Care (BSC), Venetoclax

The main objective of this study is to evaluate the efficacy of venetoclax in combination with azacitidine to improve Overall Survival (OS) in Acute Myeloid Leukemia (AML) participants compared to Best Supportive Care (BSC) when given as maintenance therapy following allogeneic stem cell transplantation (SCT). This study will have 2 parts: Part 1 (Dose Confirmation), which may include participants who are greater than or equal to 18 years old; Part 2 (Randomization) which may include participants who are greater than or equal to 12 years old. During Part 1, recommended Phase 3 dose of venetoclax in combination with azacitidine will be determined and during Part 2, the efficacy and safety of venetoclax with azacitidine (Part 2 Arm A) will be compared with BSC (Part 2 Arm B).

NCT07267234Supportive care study

PRaG-1 Plus PRaG Therapy in in Elderly Patients With Advanced Solid Tumors: A Prospective Clinical Trial (PRaG 10.0)

Phase 2 · Recruiting · Ages 18 to no limit · score 4

  • Age 84
  • Myeloid
  • Enrolling
  • Non-intensive
  • No transplant
  • Near Seattle
  • Phase 3/4

Matched population: elderly · setting: relapsed · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

PD -1/PD-L1 monoclonal antibody

The goal of this clinical trial is to learn if a combination treatment using PRaG-1 Cordycepin Tablets with radiation therapy, immune-boosting injections, and immunotherapy drugs can help patients with advanced solid tumors. It will also assess safety. The main questions it aims to answer are: Does this treatment improve immune function and slow tumor growth? What side effects or risks occur during treatment? Participants will: Take PRaG-1 Cordycepin Tablets (a natural compound derived from Cordyceps fungus) orally: higher dose for 7 days before radiation, then lower daily dose for 2 weeks Receive targeted radiation therapy to the tumor area (5-12 Gy total in 2-3 sessions) Get daily immune-boosting injections (GM-CSF) for 7 days starting with radiation Receive immunotherapy drugs (PD-1/PD-L1 inhibitors) within one week after radiation Have blood drawn and small tumor tissue samples taken before and after the first two treatment cycles for immune analysis All participants will receive this combination treatment; there is no placebo or alternative treatment group in this study.

Summary and interventions

Azacitidine, Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Gilteritinib, Venetoclax

This phase II MyeloMATCH treatment trial compares the usual treatment of azacitidine and venetoclax to the combination treatment of azacitidine, venetoclax and gilteritinib in treating older and unfit patients with acute myeloid leukemia and FLT3 mutations. Azacitidine is a drug that is absorbed into DNA and leads to the activation of cancer suppressor genes, which are genes that help control cell growth. Gilteritinib is in a class of medications called kinase inhibitors. It works by blocking the action of a certain naturally occurring substance that may be needed to help cancer cells multiply. This study may help doctors find out if these different approaches are better than the usual approaches. To decide if they are better, the study doctors are looking to see if the study drugs lead to a higher percentage of patients achieving a deeper remission compared to the usual approach.

Near Seattle Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-First Hill, Seattle (0 mi); University of Washington Medical Center - Montlake, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Edmonds, Edmonds (14 mi)

Matched drugs: gilteritinib, quizartinib, venetoclax, azacitidine, decitabine

Summary and interventions

Allogeneic Hematopoietic Stem Cell Transplantation, Azacitidine, Best Practice, Biopsy Procedure, Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Busulfan, Chest Radiography, Computed Tomography, Cytarabine, Daunorubicin

This MyeloMATCH Master Screening and Reassessment Protocol (MSRP) evaluates the use of a screening tool and specific laboratory tests to help improve participants' ability to register to clinical trials throughout the course of their myeloid cancer (acute myeloid leukemia or myelodysplastic syndrome) treatment. This study involves testing patients' bone marrow and blood for certain biomarkers. A biomarker (sometimes called a marker) is any molecule in the body that can be measured. Doctors look at markers to learn what is happening in the body. Knowing about certain markers can give doctors more information about what is driving the cancer and how to treat it. Testing patients' bone marrow and blood will show doctors if patients have markers that specific drugs can target. The marker testing in this study will let doctors know if they can match patients with a treatment study (myeloMATCH clinical trial) that tests treatment for the type of cancer they have or continue standard of care treatment with their doctor on the Tier Advancement Pathway (TAP).

Ziftomenib is an investigational drug in development for the treatment of patients with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) with eligible genetic alterations. Ziftomenib is a type of therapy known to target the menin pathway in cancer cells. This protocol has 2 separate studies that will investigate the benefits and risks of adding ziftomenib to standard-of-care (SOC) AML treatments in patients with certain genetic mutations who have not received any treatment for their AML. In the first study, the Nonintensive Therapy Study, older patients or those with serious medical problems will receive the SOC therapies venetoclax (ven) and azacitidine (aza), plus either ziftomenib or a placebo. In the second study, the Intensive Therapy Study, medically fit patients will receive (a) the SOC therapies cytarabine and daunorubicin, plus either ziftomenib or a placebo during a first treatment phase called induction, (b) cytarabine plus either ziftomenib or a placebo during a second treatment phase called consolidation, and (c) ziftomenib or a placebo during a third treatment phase called maintenance. Near Seattle Seattle Cancer Care Alliance, Seattle (0 mi); Oregon Health and Science University, Portland (145 mi)

Matched drugs: venetoclax, azacitidine, decitabine · population: ineligible for intensive · setting: refractory · supportive: transfusions, infection

Summary and interventions

ASTX030 (cedazuridine + azacitidine), Azacitidine, Cedazuridine, Venetoclax

Study ASTX030-01 is a multi-phase study comprising of Phases 1-3 Monotherapy arms, and Phase 1 and Phase 2 Combination Therapy arms. Phase 1 Monotherapy consists of an open-label Dose Escalation Stage (Stage A) using multiple cohorts at escalating dose levels of oral cedazuridine and azacitidine (only one study drug will be escalated at a time) followed by a Dose Expansion Stage (Stage B). Phase 2 Monotherapy is a randomized, open-label, crossover study to compare oral ASTX030 to subcutaneous (SC) azacitidine. Phase 3 Monotherapy is a randomized open-label crossover study comparing the final fixed dose of oral ASTX030 to SC azacitidine. Phase 1 Combination Therapy is an open-label, multicenter, randomized, exploratory study comparing ASTX030 and SC azacitidine in combination with venetoclax in participants with treatment-naïve AML. Phase 2 Combination Therapy is an open-label, single arm, study evaluating the efficacy, safety, pharmacokinetics (PK), and drug interactions of ASTX030 in combination with venetoclax in participants with treatment-naïve AML. The duration of this multi-phase study is approximately 8 years.

Summary and interventions

Placebo, Revumenib

Treatment of patients with newly diagnosed AML who are not eligible for intensive chemotherapy has remained an area of high unmet medical need. The combination therapy with two medicines, azacitidine and venetoclax, is the usual plan of action. This has brought significant progress in the treatment, but it nevertheless is not curative and the disease does relapse over time. Revumenib blocks a specific molecule called menin in the cell nucleus. Some types of AML are reliant on menin working properly. These are leukemia cells with a change in the DNA, i.e. a mutation in the NPM1 or KMT2A gene. Revumenib can prevent the production of these types of leukemia cells by disrupting the production of this menin. The current study investigates whether adding revumenib to the combination therapy improves the prognosis for AML patients with a mutation in the NPM1 or KMT2A gene. This is a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled clinical study where subjects will be treated until disease progression, or development of side effects or death. Matched genes: FLT3 · drugs: gilteritinib, venetoclax, azacitidine · population: unfit, older patients · supportive: supportive care, caregiver, infection

Summary and interventions

Azacitidine, Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Gilteritinib, Venetoclax

This phase II MyeloMATCH treatment trial compares the usual treatment of azacitidine and venetoclax to the combination treatment of azacitidine, venetoclax and gilteritinib in treating older and unfit patients with acute myeloid leukemia and FLT3 mutations. Azacitidine is a drug that is absorbed into DNA and leads to the activation of cancer suppressor genes, which are genes that help control cell growth. Venetoclax is in a class of medications called B-cell lymphoma-2 (BCL-2) inhibitors. It may stop the growth of cancer cells by blocking Bcl-2, a protein needed for cancer cell survival. Gilteritinib is in a class of medications called kinase inhibitors. It works by blocking the action of a certain naturally occurring substance that may be needed to help cancer cells multiply. This study may help doctors find out if these different approaches are better than the usual approaches. To decide if they are better, the study doctors are looking to see if the study drugs lead to a higher percentage of patients achieving a deeper remission compared to the usual approach.

Near Seattle Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Medical Center-First Hill, Seattle (0 mi); University of Washington Medical Center - Montlake, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Edmonds, Edmonds (14 mi)

Summary and interventions

Allogeneic Hematopoietic Stem Cell Transplantation, Azacitidine, Best Practice, Biopsy Procedure, Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Busulfan, Chest Radiography, Computed Tomography, Cytarabine, Daunorubicin

This MyeloMATCH Master Screening and Reassessment Protocol (MSRP) evaluates the use of a screening tool and specific laboratory tests to help improve participants' ability to register to clinical trials throughout the course of their myeloid cancer (acute myeloid leukemia or myelodysplastic syndrome) treatment. This study involves testing patients' bone marrow and blood for certain biomarkers. A biomarker (sometimes called a marker) is any molecule in the body that can be measured. Doctors look at markers to learn what is happening in the body. Knowing about certain markers can give doctors more information about what is driving the cancer and how to treat it. Testing patients' bone marrow and blood will show doctors if patients have markers that specific drugs can target. The marker testing in this study will let doctors know if they can match patients with a treatment study (myeloMATCH clinical trial) that tests treatment for the type of cancer they have or continue standard of care treatment with their doctor on the Tier Advancement Pathway (TAP).

Near Seattle Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center, Seattle (0 mi)

Matched setting: relapsed, refractory · supportive: infection, infections

Summary and interventions

CTX-712

The goal of this phase 1/2 multicenter, open-label, single-arm dose escalation and expansion study is to assess the safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic profile of CTX-712 in patients with relapsed/refractory (R/R) acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and higher risk myelodysplastic syndromes (HR-MDS), or MDS/MPN (including CMML). The phase 1 part of the study consists of sequential standard 3 + 3 dose escalation, where patients will receive ascending doses of CTX-712 to determine the recommended phase 2 dose (RP2D) for further clinical development. This is followed by initial expansion cohorts in AML and/or HR-MDS where patients will be treated with CTX-712 at the RP2D to gain further confidence in the selected dose level. Additional expansion cohorts may be initiated if considered necessary. After RP2D is determined, Drug-Drug-Interaction cohorts will be started. The phase 2 part of the study will commence after the RP2D has been identified and confirmed and will evaluate therapeutic activity in R/R AML or R/R HR-MDS, in addition to confirmation of the safety profile.

SEVENAZA: A Multicenter Trial Evaluating Efficacy and Safety of A Reduced Venetoclax Exposure To Seven Days Versus Standard Continuous Venetoclax Exposure Combined With Azacitidine in Treatment Naïve Subjects With Acute Myeloid Leukemia Who Are Ineligible for Intensive Induction

Phase 3 · Not yet recruiting · Ages 70 to no limit · score 9
