Second opinion (Opus 5 5) disagrees Depends on: other information. Quoted: Hypomethylating agent, venetoclax and/or any chemo-therapeutic agent for Myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS). Worth raising with her team.

Matched drugs: venetoclax, azacitidine · population: ineligible for intensive · supportive: fatigue, infection, infections

Summary and interventions

Azacitidine, Venetoclax

Combination of azacitidine (AZA) for 7 days every 28 days with a continuous daily exposure to Venetoclax (VEN), an oral bcl-2 inhibitor, is now approved for the treatment of acute myeloid leukemia (AML) in patients ineligible for intensive chemotherapy due to age (\>75 years) or comorbidities. VEN+AZA showed significant overall response rate and survival benefit but combination carries a risk of considerable toxicity (such as profound/prolonged cytopenia and infections) before but also after remission. These toxicities make it difficult to apply the recommended treatment regimen, in particular the continuous daily intake of VEN. Recent reports suggest that reducing VEN duration per cycle seems safe and feasible. We propose to investigate a reduced-intensity VEN regimen (7-day dosing/28) versus the standard continuous VEN therapy (28-day dosing/28), combined with AZA, with the primary goal of maintaining efficacy while reducing associated toxicity.

Iadademstat inhibits the LSD1 protein and may lead to inhibition of cell growth in LSD1-overexpressing cancer cells. Chemotherapy drugs, such as azacitidine, work in different ways to stop the growth of cancer cells, either by killing the cells, by stopping them from dividing, or by stopping them from spreading. Venetoclax is in a class of medications called B-cell lymphoma-2 (Bcl-2) inhibitors. It may stop the growth of cancer cells by blocking Bcl-2, a protein needed for cancer cell survival. Giving iadademstat with azacitidine and venetoclax may be safe, tolerable and/or effective in treating patients with newly diagnosed AML who cannot undergo intensive chemotherapy.

Near Seattle OHSU Knight Cancer Institute, Portland (145 mi)

Matched genes: FLT3 · drugs: azacitidine · setting: maintenance, MRD · supportive: transfusion, transfusions, infection, infections

Summary and interventions

Allogeneic Hematopoietic Stem Cell Transplantation, Azacitidine, Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Cyclophosphamide, Echocardiography Test, Electronic Health Record Review, Multigated Acquisition Scan, Mycophenolate Mofetil, Reduced-Intensity Transplant Conditioning Procedure, Ruxolitinib

This phase I trial studies the side effects and best dose of ruxolitinib (Rux) therapy alone (monotherapy) followed by Rux plus azacitidine (AZA) maintenance therapy and to see how well it works in treating patients with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) who are undergoing reduced intensity allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (alloHSCT). AlloHSCT provides the only chance for cure for many patients with AML. AlloHSCT is a procedure in which a person receives blood-forming stem cells (cells from which all blood cells develop) from a genetically similar, but not identical, donor. This is often a sister or brother, but could be an unrelated donor. One of the common reasons for death after an alloHSCT is graft versus host disease (GVHD), which occurs when the transplanted cells from the donor attacks the recipient's normal cells. Ruxolitinib is in a class of medications called kinase inhibitors. It works to treat GVHD by blocking the signals of the cells that cause GVHD. Azacitidine is in a class of medications called demethylation agents. It works by helping the bone marrow to produce normal blood cells and by killing abnormal cells in the bone marrow. Near Seattle Fred Hutch/University of Washington Cancer Consortium, Seattle (0 mi)

Matched drugs: venetoclax, azacitidine, decitabine · setting: relapsed, refractory, MRD · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Cladribine, Cyclophosphamide, Cyclosporine, Cytarabine, Echocardiography Test, Filgrastim, Fludarabine, Hematopoietic Cell Transplantation, Idarubicin

This phase I trial studies the best dose of total body irradiation when given with cladribine, cytarabine, filgrastim, and mitoxantrone (CLAG-M) or idarubicin, fludarabine, cytarabine and filgrastim (FLAG-Ida) chemotherapy reduced-intensity conditioning regimen before stem cell transplant in treating patients with acute myeloid leukemia, myelodysplastic syndrome, or chronic myelomonocytic leukemia that has come back (relapsed) or does not respond to treatment (refractory). Giving chemotherapy and total body irradiation before a donor peripheral blood stem cell transplant helps kill cancer cells in the body and helps make room in the patient's bone marrow for new blood-forming cells (stem cells) to grow. When the healthy stem cells from a donor are infused into a patient, they may help the patient's bone marrow make more healthy cells and platelets and may help destroy any remaining cancer cells. Sometimes the transplanted cells from a donor can attack the body's normal cells called graft versus host disease. Giving cyclophosphamide, cyclosporine, and mycophenolate mofetil after the transplant may stop this from happening.

Matched drugs: venetoclax · population: unfit, older patients, non-intensive · supportive: supportive care, palliative, fatigue, infection

Summary and interventions

Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Collection, Electronic Health Record Review, Questionnaire Administration

This clinical trial studies whether less fit adults with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) or myeloid neoplasms are willing to let a computer program decide (randomization) whether they receive lower- or higher-intensity chemotherapy. Historically, treatment decision-making for patients with AML or myeloid neoplasms has divided patients into two categories, with patients considered fit receiving intensive "curative" chemotherapy, and patients considered unfit, such as older patients with a higher risk of early death from therapy, receiving non-intensive "palliative" therapy or no therapy. With the introduction of new treatment agents, it has become difficult to determine the difference between intensive and non-intensive therapy, especially for patients considered unfit for whom treatment-related side effects remain a concern. Treatment intensity is best identified through randomized trials but often patients are unwilling to undergo randomization due to preset beliefs. However, with improved supportive care and the awareness that new treatment agents may have similar risks as intensive therapy, it may be possible that more patients are willing to be randomized. Near Seattle Fred Hutch/University of Washington Cancer Consortium, Seattle (0 mi)

Matched supportive: quality of life, transfusion, transfusions

Summary and interventions

Activity Tracking, Biospecimen Collection, Cognitive Assessment, Electronic Health Record Review, Packed Red Blood Cell Transfusion, Quality-of-Life Assessment

This clinical trial evaluates the effects of hemoglobin threshold-specific packed red blood cell (PRBC) transfusions on quality of life and functional outcomes in patients who have undergone chemotherapy or an allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplant for a high-grade myeloid neoplasm, acute myeloid leukemia, or B acute lymphoblastic lymphoma/leukemia. Some types of chemotherapy and stem cell transplants can induce low platelet counts and/or anemia that requires PRBC transfusions. Given critical shortages in blood supply, and risks associated with transfusion of PRBC, there has been much investigation into the "minimum" hemoglobin level that effectively balances safety and toxicity in patients. This clinical trial evaluates the effects of giving PRBC transfusions based on a more restrictive hemoglobin threshold (\> 7 gm/dL) compared to a more liberal hemoglobin threshold (\> 9 gm/dL) on quality of life and functional outcomes. A more restrictive threshold may be just as effective at maintaining patient quality of life and function while decreasing side effects from blood transfusions and helping to conserve blood supply resources.

Near Seattle Fred Hutch/University of Washington Cancer Consortium, Seattle (0 mi)

Matched population: older adults · supportive: exercise

Summary and interventions

Aerobic Exercise, Biospecimen Collection, Electronic Health Record Review, Interview, Resistance Training, Survey Administration

This clinical trial studies how well a home-based exercise intervention called Cardiorespiratory Fitness in Bone Marrow Transplant Program (CAREFit-BMT) works in improving heart function among patients with high risk acute myeloid leukemia (AML) undergoing stem cell transplant. Older adults and those with other medical conditions are at a higher risk for complications in the heart and blood vessels (cardiovascular). Older age at transplantation has been associated with nearly all cardiovascular complications occurring after stem cell transplant. This is likely explained by the structural and functional changes that occur in aging hearts as well as the larger burden of cardiovascular risk factors such as diabetes, high blood pressure, and obesity. CAREFit-BMT is a "prehabilitation" program, or exercise initiated prior to intensive therapy, that consists of aerobic exercises and strength training. CAREFit-BMT may be able to increase the likelihood of safe and successful transition to stem cell transplant in patients with high risk AML.

Registries, sample collections and quality-of-life studies that follow patients without assigning a treatment. Few take older adults with AML; they are listed so nothing local is missed.

Results posted 2026-10-06 results tab

Matched genes: FLT3 · drugs: venetoclax, azacitidine, decitabine · population: older patients · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration and Biopsy, Cytarabine, Daunorubicin, Decitabine and Cedazuridine, Venetoclax

This phase Ib/II trial studies the effects of ASTX727 (decitabine and cedazuridine) in combination with venetoclax in treating patients with higher-risk acute myeloid leukemia patients who do not have a change in the gene called fms-like tyrosine kinase 3 (FLT3). Decitabine is in a class of medications called hypomethylation agents. It works by helping the bone marrow produce normal blood cells and by killing abnormal cells in the bone marrow. Cedazuridine is an enzyme inhibitor. It helps to increase the amount of decitabine in the body so that the medication will have a greater effect. Venetoclax is in a class of medications called B-cell lymphoma-2 (BCL-2) inhibitors. Venetoclax may stop the growth of cancer cells by blocking BCL-2, a protein needed for cancer cell survival. Venetoclax and decitabine are commonly given together for older patients with AML ASTX727 (a pill form of decitabine + cedazuridine) has been found to be equal to decitabine (given intravenously), and this part of the study is to confirm that venetoclax and ASTX727 is as safe as venetoclax and decitabine given intravenously. This study allows for lowering doses of study drugs to assure the dose chosen for the

Results posted 2026-06-08 results tab

Matched drugs: azacitidine, decitabine · population: unfit · setting: relapsed, refractory · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Azacitidine, Decitabine, Laboratory Biomarker Analysis, Pharmacological Study, Recombinant EphB4-HSA Fusion Protein

This trial studies the side effects of recombinant EphB4-HSA fusion protein when given together with azacitidine or decitabine in treating patients with myelodysplastic syndrome, chronic myelomonocytic leukemia, or acute myeloid leukemia that has come back or has not responded to previous treatment with a hypomethylating agent. Recombinant EphB4-HSA fusion protein may stop the growth of tumor cells by blocking some of the enzymes needed for cell growth. Hypomethylating agents, such as azacitidine and decitabine, slow down genes that promote cell growth and can kill cells that are dividing rapidly. Giving recombinant EphB4-HSA fusion protein together with azacitidine or decitabine may work better in treating patients with myelodysplastic syndrome, chronic myelomonocytic leukemia, or acute myeloid leukemia.

Results posted 2026-06-04 results tab

Matched drugs: venetoclax, azacitidine · setting: maintenance · supportive: supportive care, infection

Summary and interventions

Azacitidine, Best Supportive Care (BSC), Venetoclax

The main objective of this study is to evaluate the efficacy of venetoclax in combination with azacitidine to improve Overall Survival (OS) in Acute Myeloid Leukemia (AML) participants compared to Best Supportive Care (BSC) when given as maintenance therapy following allogeneic stem cell transplantation (SCT). This study will have 2 parts: Part 1 (Dose Confirmation), which may include participants who are greater than or equal to 18 years old; Part 2 (Randomization) which may include participants who are greater than or equal to 12 years old. During Part 1, recommended Phase 3 dose of venetoclax in combination with azacitidine will be determined and during Part 2, the efficacy and safety of venetoclax with azacitidine (Part 2 Arm A) will be compared with BSC (Part 2 Arm B).

Near Seattle Swedish Medical Center-First Hill, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Edmonds, Edmonds (14 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Issaquah, Issaquah (15 mi)

Matched drugs: azacitidine, decitabine · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Decitabine and Cedazuridine, Enasidenib

This phase II MyeloMATCH treatment trial compares the usual treatment of cedazuridine-decitabine (ASTX727) to the combination treatment of ASTX727 and enasidenib in treating patients with higher-risk, IDH2-mutated myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS). ASTX727 is a combination of two drugs, decitabine and cedazuridine. Cedazuridine is in a class of medications called cytidine deaminase inhibitors. It prevents the breakdown of decitabine, making it more available in the body so that decitabine will have a greater effect. Decitabine is in a class of medications called hypomethylation agents. It works by helping the bone marrow produce normal blood cells and by killing abnormal cells in the bone marrow. Enasidenib is an enzyme inhibitor that may stop the growth of cells by blocking some of the enzymes needed for cell growth. Giving ASTX727 in combination with enasidenib may be effective in treating patients with higher-risk IDH2-mutated MDS.

Near Seattle US-Portland OR-OHSU, Portland (145 mi)

Matched drugs: venetoclax, azacitidine · population: ineligible for intensive, not eligible for intensive · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Placebo, Revumenib

Treatment of patients with newly diagnosed AML who are not eligible for intensive chemotherapy has remained an area of high unmet medical need. The combination therapy with two medicines, azacitidine and venetoclax, is the usual plan of action. This has brought significant progress in the treatment, but it nevertheless is not curative and the disease does relapse over time. Revumenib blocks a specific molecule called menin in the cell nucleus. Some types of AML are reliant on menin working properly. These are leukemia cells with a change in the DNA, i.e. a mutation in the NPM1 or KMT2A gene. Revumenib can prevent the production of these types of leukemia cells by disrupting the production of this menin. The current study investigates whether adding revumenib to the combination therapy improves the prognosis for AML patients with a mutation in the NPM1 or KMT2A gene. This is a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled clinical study where subjects will be treated until disease progression, or development of side effects or death. From the moment of inclusion of the last patient, there will be a 4-year observational follow-up study in order to register survival duration and fol

This phase II trial tests how well venetoclax with cladribine and cytarabine alternating with azacitidine and venetoclax works in treating patients with newly diagnosed monocytic acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and active signaling mutated AML. Matched drugs: venetoclax, azacitidine · population: ineligible for intensive · supportive: fatigue, infection, infections

Summary and interventions

Azacitidine, Venetoclax

Combination of azacitidine (AZA) for 7 days every 28 days with a continuous daily exposure to Venetoclax (VEN), an oral bcl-2 inhibitor, is now approved for the treatment of acute myeloid leukemia (AML) in patients ineligible for intensive chemotherapy due to age (\>75 years) or comorbidities. VEN+AZA showed significant overall response rate and survival benefit but combination carries a risk of considerable toxicity (such as profound/prolonged cytopenia and infections) before but also after remission. These toxicities make it difficult to apply the recommended treatment regimen, in particular the continuous daily intake of VEN. Recent reports suggest that reducing VEN duration per cycle seems safe and feasible. We propose to investigate a reduced-intensity VEN regimen (7-day dosing/28) versus the standard continuous VEN therapy (28-day dosing/28), combined with AZA, with the primary goal of maintaining efficacy while reducing associated toxicity.

Near Seattle Swedish Medical Center-First Hill, Seattle (0 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Edmonds, Edmonds (14 mi); Swedish Cancer Institute-Issaquah, Issaquah (15 mi)

Matched drugs: azacitidine, decitabine · supportive: infection

Summary and interventions

Biospecimen Collection, Bone Marrow Aspiration, Bone Marrow Biopsy, Decitabine and Cedazuridine, Enasidenib

This phase II MyeloMATCH treatment trial compares the usual treatment of cedazuridine-decitabine (ASTX727) to the combination treatment of ASTX727 and enasidenib in treating patients with higher-risk, IDH2-mutated myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS). ASTX727 is a combination of two drugs, decitabine and cedazuridine. Cedazuridine is in a class of medications called cytidine deaminase inhibitors. It prevents the breakdown of decitabine, making it more available in the body so that decitabine will have a greater effect. Decitabine is in a class of medications called hypomethylation agents. It works by helping the bone marrow produce normal blood cells and by killing abnormal cells in the bone marrow. Enasidenib is an enzyme inhibitor that may stop the growth of cells by blocking some of the enzymes needed for cell growth. Giving ASTX727 in combination with enasidenib may be effective in treating patients with higher-risk IDH2-mutated MDS.

Summary and interventions

Luspatercept Injection [Reblozyl]

Myelodysplastic syndromes, primarily affecting older adults, are a heterogeneous group of clonal disorders of hematopoietic stem cells characterized by ineffective hematopoiesis that manifest clinically as anemia, neutropenia, and/or thrombocytopenia of variable severity; these often result in RBC- transfusion dependent (TD) anemia, increased risk of infection, and/or hemorrhage, as well as a potential to progress to acute myeloid leukemia (AML). Lenalidomide is approved for red blood cell transfusion-dependent (RBC TD) anemia due to low-risk myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) with a chromosome 5q deletion (del5q) with or without additional cytogenetic abnormalities. About one third of patients are refractory/resistant/intolerant and will require further treatment options. Luspatercept (ACE-536), an erythroid maturation agent, is a recombinant fusion protein consisting of a modified form of the extracellular domain (ECD) of the human activin receptor type IIB (ActRIIB) linked to the Fc portion of human immunoglobulin G1 (IgG1-Fc). TAK-226

The main aim of the study is to evaluate how TAK-226 improves symptoms of transfusion-dependent anemia in Japanese patients with lower-risk myelodysplastic syndromes. During Treatment Period, the participants will come to the clinic/hospital approximately every two to four weeks.

Leflunomide is administered orally at 10 to 20 mg once daily (without a loading dose) for 14 to 21 days, as part of a 28-day treatment cycle in adult subjects with R/R MDS. After 12 cycles (study duration) responding patients can continue progression with the assigned doses.

Summary and interventions

Dapagliflozin (10mg Tab)

This study is a prospective, single-arm, phase II clinical trial designed to evaluate the efficacy and safety of dapagliflozin in improving anemia in patients with lower-risk myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS). Anemia is the most common clinical problem in patients with lower-risk MDS and often leads to fatigue, reduced quality of life, and the need for repeated blood transfusions. Current treatment options, including erythropoiesis-stimulating agents and other therapies, are not effective in all patients, and additional treatment options are needed. Dapagliflozin is a sodium-glucose cotransporter-2 (SGLT2) inhibitor that is widely used for the treatment of diabetes, heart failure, and chronic kidney disease. Previous studies have shown that SGLT2 inhibitors can increase hemoglobin levels, possibly by stimulating erythropoiesis. In this study, eligible patients will receive dapagliflozin 10 mg orally once daily for 24 weeks. The primary objective is to evaluate the hemoglobin response rate during the study period. Secondary objectives include changes in hemoglobin levels, transfusion requirements, and safety outcomes. This study aims to explore whether dapagliflozin can serve as a

NCT06923670DDX41 family and germline study

Prevalence Of Germline Gene Mutations In Patients With Myeloproliferative Neoplasms With Family History

Interventional · Not yet recruiting · Ages 18 to no limit · score 0

  • Age 84
  • Myeloid
  • Enrolling
  • Non-intensive
  • No transplant
  • Near Seattle
  • Phase 3/4
Blocking criterion Reviewed by Claude 2026-10-26
Quoted criterion, inclusion

Diagnosis of MPN (Essential Thrombocythemia, Polycythemia Vera, Myelofibrosis) confirmed according to ICC 2022 criteria

Question for her team: Since this study is for myeloproliferative neoplasms and her diagnosis is AML, is there any way she would fit this study?

Summary and interventions

NGS analysis for mutations in genes involved in familial predisposition to hematological malignancies, NGS testing

Philadelphia-negative myeloproliferative neoplasms (MPNs) occur sporadically and are due to somatic mutations in the JAK2 (Janus kinase 2), CALR (calreticulin) and MPL (thrombopoietin receptor) genes. However, data from epidemiological and family studies clearly highlight a heritable component that influences the risk of developing MPN and potentially contributes to the observed phenotypic pleiotropy. Genome-wide association studies in MPN familial clusters have identified a number of germline genetic variants associated with an increased risk of developing MPN. The strongest association discovered so far is the presence of the JAK2 46/1 haplotype and, subsequently, several studies have found additional variants in other genes, particularly in the TERT gene. The aim of the study would be to investigate the presence of germline mutations in MPN patients selected on the basis of a family history of myeloid neoplasms through the analysis of both already recognized genes and other potentially implicated ones.

Question for her team: Since this study requires a myeloproliferative neoplasm diagnosis and she has AML, does it apply to her at all?

Summary and interventions

The classic Ph-negative myeloproliferative neoplasms (MPN) are a group of clonal hematopoietic disorders caused by a dysregulated JAK/STAT signal transduction because of acquired somatic mutations of JAK2, CALR or MPL genes. They are sporadic diseases but there are several lines of evidence that support the role of germline factors in the pathogenesis of MPN: the existence of familial clustering, the presence of more than one clone in some patients, the known existence of common polymorphisms that cause predisposition to MPN. In this study, we would like to define the germline predisposition to MPN.

NCT06022016DDX41 family and germline study

LUCID: Study of Families With an Hemopathies Predisposition Related to the DDX41 Gene.

Interventional · Recruiting · Ages 18 to no limit · score 3

  • Age 84
  • Myeloid
  • Enrolling
  • Non-intensive
  • No transplant
  • Near Seattle
  • Phase 3/4

Matched genes: DDX41

Summary and interventions

For each person (index case or related) included in this study:

This is a multicenter, interventional, historico-prospective cohort pilot study aimed at specifying the phenotype of subjects carrying a constitutional familial DDX41 mutation, with a view to eventually publishing oncogenetic recommendations for carriers of this mutation. The main objective of the LUCID project is to assess the cumulative risk of hematological diseases as a function of age in DDX41 germline mutation carriers. This study will be carried out in two stages: Stage 1: Inclusion of index cases in an oncogenetic consultation (salivary test, completion of an health self-questionnaire and collection of contact details for the related cases). Stage 2: Proposition of participation to family members, by correspondence, and determination of carrier or non-carrier status of the constitutional familial DDX41 mutation (based on a salivary test). A maximum of 210 index case patients and 700 family member will be included in this study.

Question for her team: This trial requires planned intensive chemotherapy or transplant; since her treatment is non-intensive, does this trial apply to her at all?

Second opinion (Opus 5 5): agrees.

Near Seattle Fred Hutch/University of Washington Cancer Consortium, Seattle (0 mi)

Matched supportive: quality of life, transfusion, transfusions

Summary and interventions

Activity Tracking, Biospecimen Collection, Cognitive Assessment, Electronic Health Record Review, Packed Red Blood Cell Transfusion, Quality-of-Life Assessment

This clinical trial evaluates the effects of hemoglobin threshold-specific packed red blood cell (PRBC) transfusions on quality of life and functional outcomes in patients who have undergone chemotherapy or an allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplant for a high-grade myeloid neoplasm, acute myeloid leukemia, or B acute lymphoblastic lymphoma/leukemia. Some types of chemotherapy and stem cell transplants can induce low platelet counts and/or anemia that requires PRBC transfusions. Given critical shortages in blood supply, and risks associated with transfusion of PRBC, there has been much investigation into the "minimum" hemoglobin level that effectively balances safety and toxicity in patients. This clinical trial evaluates the effects of giving PRBC transfusions based on a more restrictive hemoglobin threshold (\> 7 gm/dL) compared to a more liberal hemoglobin threshold (\> 9 gm/dL) on quality of life and functional outcomes. A more restrictive threshold may be just as effective at maintaining patient quality of life and function while decreasing side effects from blood transfusions and helping to conserve blood supply resources.

Elritercept, Epoetin Alfa

NCT07117422Supportive care study

Venetoclax-Decitabine in Untreated Elderly/Unfit AML

Interventional · Recruiting · Ages 65 to no limit · score 15

  • Near Seattle

Blocking criterion Reviewed by Claude 2026-10-26
Quoted criterion, inclusion

No prior AML therapy, except:Hydroxyurea、Low-dose cytarabine (<1.0 g/day)

Question for her team: This trial requires no prior AML therapy beyond hydroxyurea or low-dose cytarabine. Does her HMA plus venetoclax start rule it out?

Matched drugs: venetoclax, azacitidine, decitabine · population: unfit, elderly, low-intensity · setting: measurable residual disease, MRD · supportive: infections

Summary and interventions

VEN + DEC

Acute myeloid leukemia (AML) is a highly fatal malignancy in China, with particularly poor outcomes in elderly patients. Low-intensity regimens yield low remission rates, and median overall survival (OS) typically remains under 6-9 months. Venetoclax (VEN) combined with hypomethylating agents (azacitidine or decitabine（DEC）) has emerged as a first-line therapy for these patients, significantly improving response rates and survival. However, challenges persist, including suboptimal complete remission (CR) rates, low Measurable Residual Disease（MRD） negativity, and tolerability issues with prolonged use. Recent studies suggest that a 3-day decitabine regimen combined with VEN may enhance efficacy and tolerability. Building on prior evidence and our institutional experience, we propose this study to evaluate an optimized dosing strategy of VEN plus decitabine in treatment-naïve elderly or chemotherapy-ineligible AML patients, aiming to further improve clinical outcomes.

Matched drugs: venetoclax · population: older adults, lower-intensity · supportive: supportive care, geriatric assessment

Summary and interventions

Active Default

The goal of this clinical trial is to test a way of delivering supportive care to adults 60 years and older who are starting venetoclax-based treatment for newly diagnosed acute myeloid leukemia (AML). Older adults with AML often have aging-related needs, such as problems with mobility, nutrition, mood, memory, or medications, that are not always addressed in a consistent way. In this study, a health questionnaire and short in-person checks (a geriatric assessment) are used to find these needs early, and matching supportive care orders are prepared in the electronic health record and sent to the treating doctor, who decides whether to place each one. This approach is called Active Default. The main questions the study aims to answer are: * How often are the prepared supportive care orders signed by treating doctors (implementation fidelity)? * Can each step of the process, from completing the assessment to receiving supportive care services, be carried out as planned? Participants will: * Complete questionnaires and short in-person checks of walking, balance, strength, memory, mood, and medications at the start of treatment * Receive supportive care services (for example physica

EPIAMLINF: Epidemiology of Infection in Acute Myeloid Leukemia (AML)

Question for her team: Does this infection study include her, given she has started HMA plus venetoclax and when her AML was diagnosed (after 01/03/2025)?

Possible requirement: relapsed or refractory disease Her answers: prior treatment: hma_venetoclax_started
Matched text, inclusion criteria

* AML patients treated with induction treatment, consolidation treatment, or relapsed/refractory (for these latter patients the first diagnosis must not be prior to March 01, 2025)

Found by a text pattern, not reviewed. Check the full criteria on ClinicalTrials.gov.

Matched drugs: venetoclax · setting: relapsed, refractory · supportive: supportive care, infection, infections

Summary and interventions

data analysis

In recent years, multiple factors have strongly impacted the epidemiology of infections in patients with acute myeloid leukemia. On the one hand, the availability of new effective antileukemic drugs (i.e. venetoclax, FLT-3 inhibitors, CPX-351) have expanded the pharmacological armamentarium. On the other hand, first, many of them inhere drug-drug interactions with azoles and fluoroquinolones, facing clinicians with the choice of whether to administer antimicrobial prophylaxis or not. Secondly, there is an increase in infections due to multi-resistant agents from both the bacterial and fungal field. Third, the onset of a viral pandemic that had high relevance in these patients in terms of morbidity and mortality. The aim of this survey is to collect information on the largest possible sample of patients with AML during induction/consolidation/relapsed-refractory treatment, regarding bacterial, viral, fungal infections. We will evaluate the incidence of the various types of infection in relation to the type of treatment that patients will undergo, in order to identify what should be the best antimicrobial prophylactic approach in each subset of patients.

Found by a text pattern, not reviewed. Check the full criteria on ClinicalTrials.gov.

Matched setting: relapsed, refractory · supportive: palliative, caregiver, caregivers, advance care planning

Summary and interventions

Advance consultation concerning your life and treatment (ACT )

Patients diagnosed with hematologic cancer are at substantial risk of dying, as 5-year survival among patients with acute myeloid leukemia is 20 % and only every second patient treated for incurable myeloma lives 5 years after date of diagnosis. Nevertheless, many overestimate their prognosis, and value of therapy. Patients with hematological cancers frequently have poor end of life outcomes, such as high treatment activity close to death, where clinical effects are doubtful, and low utilization of palliative care. Prognostic awareness and end of life (EOL) issues have urgency in the communication between patients, their caregiving relatives, and clinicians, in order to avoid futile treatments and suffering at EOL. Inspired by advanced care planning, the investigators developed the concept "Advance Consultations Concerning participants Life and Treatment" (ACT) in collaboration with a group consisting of hematologists, nurses, patients, and caregivers. The ACT concept consists of an 8-hour training day for clinicians, clinical tools, system changes, and preparation material for patients and caregivers prior to the consultation. 2026-11-02accepted (2026-11-02)

Read Health Canada's Clinical Trials Database for AML trials authorized in Canada

Gap Canadian sites count as local (Vancouver is 119 miles away). Health Canada authorizes every drug trial run in Canada, sometimes before the sponsor registers it on ClinicalTrials.gov, so a new Canadian AML trial could appear there first.

Would add A free public API (health-products.canada.ca/api/clinical-trial/): the full protocol list (9,762 protocols, about 6 MB, one minute to download), filtered by the AML conditions (ids 719, 506, 944, 165) and by title, with the drug list per protocol. Records have no NCT number and no sites, so each would be matched to ClinicalTrials.gov by drug and title.

Would have found A live test on 2026-11-02 found 126 AML protocols, 6 authorized in the last 12 months. Five match trials already tracked: IPN60340 with azacitidine and venetoclax (NCT07623187), GAVE001 gilteritinib with azacitidine and venetoclax after venetoclax resistance (NCT07755202, Vancouver, which excludes FLT3-mutated disease), pivekimab sunirine in children, revumenib with intensive chemotherapy, and the ALC.10 / MM1OA-EA02 MyeloMATCH study (NCT06317649). One, vicrimenib (BN104) protocol BN104-102, has no clear ClinicalTrials.gov match; the BN104 studies found there (NCT07101497, NCT07559695, both new to the watch) are in China and do not match it. Nothing found that the main search had missed for her.

Effort: Medium: a slow bulk download and fuzzy matching to registry records, for a source that has so far only repeated ClinicalTrials.gov.

2026-11-01accepted (2026-11-01)

Track observational studies (registries, sample and quality-of-life studies) near Seattle that take AML

Gap Every search in the watch is limited to interventional studies, so registries, biobank studies, geriatric-assessment and quality-of-life studies that enroll older AML patients without assigning a treatment never appear.

Would add One ClinicalTrials.gov stream: AML (or leukemia, MDS, myeloid neoplasm, hematologic malignancy) with study type observational, enrolling, within 150 miles of Seattle.

Would have found A live test on 2026-11-01 found 3 enrolling observational studies with AML as a condition near Seattle: NCT01351545 (cord blood unit distribution for transplant), NCT05702645 (Down syndrome survivors, ages 6 to 39) and NCT05334069 (already tracked, ages 40 to 75). Widening the condition to leukemia, MDS and hematologic malignancy gave 24, of which 18 admit older adults; none is specific to older AML patients or her treatment. The nearest are general sample collections such as NCT00900198 and NCT06295965 (clonal hematopoiesis after cancer therapy). Nothing found that would add a question for her oncologist.

Effort: Small, but not recommended: the yield near Seattle is close to zero.

2026-10-31accepted (2026-10-31)

Search for studies on stopping, shortening or de-escalating venetoclax, in any design

Gap Questions about how long she stays on venetoclax and azacitidine will come up if she responds. Studies on stopping or shortening treatment could be phase 1, phase "not applicable" or observational, and the main search would miss them.

Would add One ClinicalTrials.gov stream: AML with venetoclax and terms such as discontinuation, treatment-free, de-escalation, fixed or limited duration, any phase or study type, enrolling.

Would have found A live test on 2026-10-31 found 66 enrolling studies, and 52 are already in data/trials.json, including every de-escalation study that fits her setting: NCT06511882 (stopping HMA and venetoclax with negative residual disease), NCT07437950 (MyeloMATCH treatment lengths), NCT07407660 (shortened venetoclax for elderly patients), NCT06557421 and NCT07867626. The 14 untracked are mostly phase 1 studies away from Seattle (for example NCT06429449, mitoxantrone for venetoclax-resistant AML, up to 80), two observational studies (NCT05587049, NCT06295029) and NCT07521124 (maintenance, up to 80). Low yield: the main search already covers this question.

Effort: Small, but not recommended: the main search already returns the relevant studies.

2026-10-30accepted (2026-10-31)

Search trials filed under MDS or "myeloid neoplasm" that also enroll AML

Gap Some trials list only myelodysplastic syndrome, CMML or "myeloid neoplasm" as the condition but accept AML in their criteria (for example MDS/AML with 10 to 29% blasts). The AML condition search could miss them.

Would add One ClinicalTrials.gov stream: condition myelodysplastic, myeloid neoplasm or chronic myelomonocytic, interventional phase 2 or 3, enrolling, kept only when the criteria text mentions AML.

Would have found A live test on 2026-10-30 found 237 enrolling phase 2/3 trials worldwide, 109 not in data/trials.json. Only 20 of those mention AML in their criteria, and 11 have no age cap below 84; most are anemia trials for lower-risk MDS (NCT07516847, NCT05924100, NCT06847867), and the closest to her are NCT07672769 (bexmarilimab with azacitidine) and NCT07011186 (TQB3909 with azacitidine), neither near Seattle. Near Seattle there were 30, 6 untracked, and the only one that mentions AML is NCT05320198 (DISC-0974 for anemia in myelofibrosis or MDS, at UW and OHSU), which is not about treating AML. Low yield: the AML condition search already catches the trials that matter.

Effort: Small: one stream entry and a criteria-text filter. Not recommended now; worth a retest if her diagnosis is reclassified as MDS/AML.

2026-10-29accepted (2026-10-31)

Read drug-label versions from DailyMed, and watch every AML-indicated label

Gap The label check asks openFDA for 7 brands. openFDA has no record of XOSPATA (gilteritinib), so the watch has never seen a gilteritinib label even though profile.json lists it. It also watches only her current and likely drugs, so a new AML approval would not show up.

Would add Ask DailyMed's free SPL service (dailymed.nlm.nih.gov/dailymed/services/v2/spls.json) for the label version of each brand in label_brands, falling back from openFDA. Once a month, also list every label whose indication names acute myeloid leukemia (openFDA, 38 labels across 25 brands today) and report brands that are new to the list.

Would have found A live test on 2026-10-29: DailyMed has XOSPATA label version 11, published 2026-09-21, which the watch missed; for the other 6 brands DailyMed and openFDA agree (for example VENCLEXTA version 283). Of the 25 AML-indicated brands in openFDA, 19 are not in data/fda.json. The ones that matter for open questions are the menin inhibitors REVUFORJ (revumenib, version 5, 2026-02-20) and KOMZIFTI (ziftomenib, version 1, 2025-12-16), which bear on the NPM1 and KMT2A question, and the IDH inhibitors TIBSOVO, REZLIDHIA and IDHIFA, which bear on the IDH1 and IDH2 question. The rest are chemotherapy and growth-factor labels.

Effort: Small: a DailyMed fallback in fda.py, a monthly AML-indication listing, a test with a recorded fixture, and a short list on the page.

2026-10-28accepted (2026-10-31)

Track AML studies registered with phase "not applicable" (treatment-strategy, digital and care-delivery trials)

Gap The main search keeps phases 2, 3 and 4, and the extra streams cover phase 1 near Seattle, supportive care and a few others. Interventional studies that ClinicalTrials.gov files as phase "NA" fall through: strategy trials comparing treatment intensities, care-delivery programs and digital tools, which are often the studies built for older, less fit patients.

Would add One more ClinicalTrials.gov stream: condition acute myeloid leukemia, interventional, AREA[Phase]NA, recruiting or not yet recruiting, ranked like the rest. Near-Seattle hits are the useful ones; the rest of the world is mostly single-centre studies from China and Europe.

Would have found A live test on 2026-10-28 found 84 enrolling phase-NA interventional AML studies, 62 of them not in data/trials.json. Near Seattle (150 miles): 5, 2 not tracked. NCT07094750 at Fred Hutch/University of Washington randomizes less fit adults with AML (no upper age limit; ECOG 0 to 2 at 75 or older; at least one unfitness criterion) between lower- and higher-intensity treatment; it started 2026-10-13 and probably wants patients before treatment, so it may not fit now, but it is the closest match to her population near Seattle the watch has missed. NCT06472128 tests a digital app for patients with acute leukemia (adults to 120 years). Further afield: NCT07490288 (venetoclax, azacitidine and liposomal mitoxantrone for newly diagnosed AML, up to 100 years).

Effort: Small: one stream entry in profile.json (filter.advanced with AREA[Phase]NA, no phase filter), a test, and a label on the page.

2026-10-28accepted (2026-10-28)

Catch new papers and congress abstracts before PubMed indexes them, through OpenAlex

Gap PubMed indexes many journals days to weeks after publication and leaves out most congress abstracts. Recent clinical reports on venetoclax and azacitidine in older patients reach the watch late or never.

Would add One OpenAlex search (free REST API, no key) for AML with venetoclax, azacitidine, FLT3, DDX41 or DNMT3A over the last 30 days, keeping only works without a PMID or DOI already stored, and labelled as not yet in PubMed. A title filter on clinical terms would keep out most laboratory abstracts.

Would have found A live test on 2026-10-28 over the last 30 days returned 1,202 works. Of the first 200, 181 were not in data/papers.json and 123 had no PMID. Most on point for her: an all-oral triplet of decitabine-cedazuridine, venetoclax and gilteritinib for newly diagnosed FLT3-mutated AML (Blood Cancer Journal, 2026-10-13), azacitidine with low-dose venetoclax in elderly newly diagnosed unfit AML (Clinical and Experimental Medicine, 2026-10-26), and a congress abstract finding no QTc prolongation with bleximenib (Hematology, Transfusion and Cell Therapy). Noise: many laboratory abstracts (Experimental Hematology), regional journals and a cost-effectiveness study. Too many to read without a clinical-terms filter or Claude triage.

Effort: Medium: an openalex.py fetcher modelled on europepmc.py, a DOI and title dedupe against the paper store, and a clinical-terms filter tuned against a week of results.

2026-10-28accepted (2026-10-28)

Show curated drug evidence for her variants from CIViC

Gap ClinVar says whether a variant is harmful, not which drugs it responds to. The watch has no curated link from her FLT3 changes to the evidence behind FLT3 inhibitors.

Would add A CIViC lookup (free GraphQL API, civicdb.org) of accepted evidence items for each of her variants in AML, shown as a small table of drug, evidence level and source, refreshed weekly.

Would have found A live test on 2026-10-28 found accepted evidence for FLT3-ITD (33 items, 30 in AML) and FLT3 D835Y (6 items, 5 in AML), including FLT3-ITD sensitivity to gilteritinib at level A (EID 7728, PMID 31665578) and D835Y sensitivity to gilteritinib (EID 8108, PMID 27908881). It also has an entry for the ITD and D835Y combination she carries. 23 cited PMIDs are not in data/papers.json, but they date from 2012 to 2019, so they add background rather than news. There was nothing for DNMT3A R882H or her DDX41 variants.

Effort: Small: a civic.py fetcher with one GraphQL query per variant, a profile list of variant names, and a table on the page.

2026-10-27accepted (2026-10-28)

Track expanded-access programs for AML drugs

Gap Every search asks for interventional trials, so expanded-access (compassionate use) programs never appear. These let a patient who cannot join a trial receive an unapproved drug through her own doctor, and they matter most for older patients excluded by trial age limits or prior treatment.

Would add One ClinicalTrials.gov search (free API v2) for AML studies of type EXPANDED_ACCESS with status available or temporarily unavailable, listed in their own short section with the drug and the sponsor contact link.

Would have found A live test on 2026-10-27 found 25 AML expanded-access records, 6 of them currently available and none in data/trials.json. Most on point for her: NCT05593185 (galinpepimut-S, a WT1 vaccine for AML in remission, a setting she may reach) and NCT05738538 (ziftomenib, a menin inhibitor, relevant only if she is NPM1-mutated or KMT2A-rearranged). The others are NCT03123029 (venetoclax, which she already receives), NCT07346105 (Orca-T cell therapy for transplant), NCT01881334 (T-cell-depleted haploidentical transplant) and NCT06915766 (bezuclastinib for systemic mastocytosis, a near miss on the condition search). About 1 or 2 useful programs, changing slowly.

Effort: Small: one more stream in profile.json with a study-type filter and its own statuses, and a short page section.

2026-10-27accepted (2026-10-27)

Watch for AML trials of her drugs that post results on ClinicalTrials.gov

Gap The watch tracks trials while they recruit and drops them from view once they close. Completed or stopped trials of venetoclax, azacitidine or gilteritinib often post their results on ClinicalTrials.gov months before a paper appears, and some never publish at all.

Would add One ClinicalTrials.gov search (free API v2) for AML trials of venetoclax, azacitidine or gilteritinib with results first posted in the last 6 months, listed under Recent changes with a link to the results tab.

Would have found A live test on 2026-10-27 found 5 trials with results posted since 2026-04-27, 4 of them not in data/trials.json: NCT04161885 (venetoclax with azacitidine versus another treatment, terminated, results 2026-06-04), NCT03573024 (venetoclax and azacitidine in adults under 60, completed), NCT03146871 (EphB4-HSA with azacitidine or decitabine for relapsed AML, terminated) and NCT06508489 (SAR443579 NK-cell engager combinations, terminated, results 2026-10-19). The fifth, NCT04817241 (oral decitabine-cedazuridine with venetoclax), is already tracked. Low volume, under one a month; NCT04161885 is the only one close to her treatment.

Effort: Small: one more stream in profile.json with a results-posted filter, and a results link on the page.

2026-10-26accepted (2026-10-26)

Search preprints (medRxiv, bioRxiv, Research Square) through Europe PMC

Gap PubMed only covers published journal articles, so findings that appear first as preprints are missed. Clinical results often show up as preprints weeks or months before a journal prints them.

Would add One Europe PMC search (SRC:PPR, free REST API, no key) for AML together with venetoclax, azacitidine, FLT3, DDX41 or DNMT3A over the last 30 days, stored alongside the papers and deduplicated by DOI once a preprint is published.

Would have found A live test on 2026-10-26 over the last 30 days found 6 preprints, none in data/papers.json (preprints have no PMID). Most on point for her: PPR1342224 (medRxiv, AI models predicting response to venetoclax and azacitidine) and PPR1343426 (Preprints.org, myelodysplasia-related gene mutations in AML and their prognostic weight). Others: PPR1335267 (progression modelling), PPR1330089 (IDH1 case report), and two laboratory studies of venetoclax resistance (PPR1344032, PPR1338304). Low volume, about 6 a month, with about a third clinically relevant.

Effort: Small: a new europepmc.py fetcher modelled on pubmed.py, one query in profile.json, and a preprint label on the page.

2026-10-26accepted (2026-10-26)

Search PubMed across all clinical study designs, plus a FLT3 non-intensive search

Gap Two of the four topic searches require a phase 3 or randomized-trial article type. Phase 2 reports, real-world cohorts, follow-ups and systematic reviews on her situation are missed. A live count on 2026-10-26 found 3 papers in 30 days across the current mutation/drug and older/unfit searches.

Would add Broaden the mutation/drug search (targeted) and the older/unfit search to any clinical design, and add a search for FLT3-mutated AML treated with venetoclax, a hypomethylating agent or a FLT3 inhibitor in older, unfit or newly diagnosed patients. Supportive-care and DDX41 searches are unchanged.

Would have found A live test on 2026-10-26 over the last 30 days. targeted: 25 papers, 23 not already reported, against 2 for the current search. older_unfit: 13, 11 new, against 1. FLT3 non-intensive (new): 7, all new. Papers most on point for her: PMID 42909668 (patient-tailored azacitidine, venetoclax and gilteritinib triplet, tolerability; Annals of Hematology), PMID 42800061 (venetoclax-based regimens in newly diagnosed FLT3-mutated older or unfit AML, systematic review; Hematological Oncology), PMID 42882037 (sorafenib with venetoclax and azacitidine in newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD AML), PMID 42839073 (venetoclax-based therapy versus HMA alone in unfit patients; Leukemia), PMID 42867841 and 42835738 (real-world venetoclax outcomes). Noise: some laboratory studies match (for example PMID 42878337, 42798295). About 40 to 45 papers a month after overlap, which is readable without Claude triage.

Effort: Small: three query strings in profile.json, plus the plan's phase 3 code (single retmax=1000 search, paper store).

2026-10-25accepted (2026-10-25)

Check shortages of her likely supportive drugs

Gap The FDA check covers leukemia drugs only. Shortages of anti-infective prophylaxis or anti-nausea drugs also affect patients on venetoclax and azacitidine.

Would add Shortage, discontinuation and recall notices for posaconazole, voriconazole, fluconazole, acyclovir, valacyclovir, levofloxacin, ondansetron, prochlorperazine, allopurinol and filgrastim.

Would have found A live test on 2026-10-25 found no current shortage. There were two discontinuation notices: Teva allopurinol tablets (4 listings, February 2026) and Merck posaconazole injection (December 2025; the tablets used for prophylaxis are not listed). Low value today, but the cost is near zero.

Effort: Trivial: add names to fda.shortage_generics and fda.recall_terms in profile.json.

2026-10-25accepted (2026-10-25)

Add phase 1 trials with a site near Seattle

Gap The main search covers phase 2 to 4 only. Early-phase trials of FLT3 and venetoclax combinations run at Fred Hutch and OHSU and never appear.

Would add Phase 1 AML trials, kept only when a site is within 150 miles, ranked after every phase 2 to 4 trial. Phase 1 trials are about dose and safety, so they are mainly worth asking about once standard options are used up.

Would have found A live test on 2026-10-25 found 52 open phase 1 AML studies with a site within 150 miles. 19 of them are phase 1/2 and already come through the main search (for example NCT05520567, gilteritinib with venetoclax and azacitidine, and NCT04086264, IMGN632 with venetoclax and azacitidine). The other 33 are phase 1 only and were not tracked, including NCT05546580 (iadademstat with gilteritinib for FLT3-mutated AML, OHSU) and NCT07645469 (WT1 T cells with azacitidine for residual disease, Fred Hutch). Correction: the first version of this entry counted phase 1/2 trials as new.

Effort: Small: one more search in profile.json streams (phase 1 only, sites within 150 miles), plus a filter and tests.

Runs

31 review runs

Data runs fetch and compare the public sources twice a day on GitHub. Review runs send due trials to Claude under a personal Claude subscription. Use is shown as a share of the 5-hour session window, with the window's own reading after the run.

Schedule: data runs are set for about 00:00 and 12:00 UTC (GitHub may start them late), which is 4 PM and 4 AM Pacific in winter and 5 PM and 5 AM in summer. Each morning, at about 7 AM Pacific, a refresh on a local machine reviews new and changed trials, using at most 15% of a 5-hour session, then rewrites the review notes and republishes this page. Second opinions and retries run only on request.
